##### Whether a denied application is necessary to challenge the approval burden itself

**Holding:** A plaintiff whose concrete intended or continuing activity is subject to an enforceable approval condition may challenge that condition without first seeking and being denied permission. The supported teaching and research activities here permit review of the condition itself; relief must still correspond to the plaintiffs' actual continuing injury under the amended coverage, and no hypothetical future refusal is adjudicated.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg’s Part I, joined by every other Justice; unanimous.

**Material application:** Specific intended or continuing scholarly activity supports the condition challenge even though no plaintiff sought statutory approval and no denial is established. Website blocking is not recast as a rejected statutory request, and a submission labeled undisputed does not establish all assertions were conceded.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The amended definition governs actual continuing injury and relief. Review of the mandatory condition neither decides a future refusal nor permits merits adjudication on hypothetical jurisdiction. For the substantive rules and remedy, see First Amendment — University scholarship and approval of public equipment use, Urofsky v. Gilmore, February 20, 2001, Fourth Circuit No. 98-1481.

### Standing in districting litigation

#### Sinkfield v. Kelley, 531 U.S. 28

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 00-132 and 00-133.
**Decided:** November 27, 2000.
**Companion or consolidated matters:** The private-intervenor and Alabama-official direct appeals share the same disposition.

##### Personal exposure to the challenged electoral assignment

**Holding:** A districting plaintiff must show personal exposure to the challenged treatment, causation, and redressability. Residence in a majority-white district next to majority-minority districts and evidence that those neighboring districts influenced the map do not, without a plaintiff-specific connection, establish personal racial-assignment injury.

**Proposition-level authority:** The Court's per curiam opinion. No numerical tally or individual joins are publicly disclosed.

**Material application:** The allegations and evidence establish neighboring-design effects but no further connection to these plaintiffs' own racial assignment. They establish no independent dilution, exclusion, unequal voting weight, or inferior membership injury. Direct or circumstantial proof may establish personal exposure; no expert study, numerical racial threshold, or electoral defeat is required.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Personal unequal treatment can establish standing even if the constitutional theory later fails. Neither unusual shape nor residence is universally sufficient or universally disqualifying. The design-only merits theory and independently supported claims not presented here receive no decision.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Both judgments are vacated; the seven-district injunction is dissolved and the asserted complaint must be dismissed for lack of standing. This is jurisdictional dismissal, without map certification, a replacement map, election cancellation, or an invented alternative claim.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Johnson v. Miller, June 29, 1995, supplies the separate personal-exposure rule and permits shape evidence when connected to the claimant. Bush v. Vera, June 13, 1996, preserves the personal-stake requirement apart from substantive entitlement. Citizens for a Better Environment v. Steel Co., June 2, 1997, requires jurisdiction before contested merits adjudication. Ruhrgas AG v. Marathon Oil Co., May 17, 1999, permits bounded nonmerits sequencing, not assumed Article III injury. Shaw v. Reno, June 28, 1993, and the substantive districting holdings remain unchanged.

### Recurring election controversies after the challenged election

#### Wood v. Meadows

**Docket or dockets:** Fourth Circuit No. 99-1069; district court No. CA-94-47-D; judgment below, 207 F.3d 708. No Supreme Court docket is established.
**Decided:** June 11, 2001.

For the deadline's constitutional validity in Wood v. Meadows, June 11, 2001, Fourth Circuit No. 99-1069, see Election Law — Independent Senate nomination deadlines.

##### The continuing deadline dispute remains fit for decision

**Holding:** Completion of the 1994 election does not end this challenge to the unchanged nomination deadline, where Wood's continuing voter and candidate interests and the documented repeated litigation over prospective enforcement establish a reasonably recurring controversy on an election timetable too short for complete review. The later signature amendment does not moot the surviving deadline question.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer, directly adopts the threshold rule. Stevens and Ginsburg also reach the merits before disagreeing with the disposition.

**Material application:** The deadline itself remained unchanged, and documented repeated litigation plus Wood's continuing voter and candidate interests established recurrence. The holding does not assume a promised future candidacy from a petition allegation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The claim concerns prospective enforcement, not reopening the completed 1994 election. The separate signature-distribution amendment and contempt proceedings remain outside the accepted question.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

Norman v. Reed applies Moore v. Ogilvie to continuing, recurring election disputes that evade full review on the election timetable; the actual continuing interests and repeated litigation satisfy that rule here.

### Prospective review of recurring presidential absentee exclusion

#### Romeu v. Cohen

**Docket or dockets:** Lower-court Nos. 00-6287 and 00-6303; no Supreme Court docket supplied.
**Decided:** 2002-02-19.

For the statutory floor, equality rules and prospective remedies in Romeu v. Cohen, February 19, 2002, lower-court Nos. 00-6287 and 00-6303, see Election Law — Equal access to a retained former-state presidential franchise.

##### Reviewability of Romeu's distinct prospective federal and state claims

**Holding:** Completion of the 2000 election does not extinguish Romeu's recurring claim to prospective presidential absentee eligibility.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Souter's opinion, joined by all nine Justices, controls.

**Material application:** Personal exclusion under continuing classifications is redressable through the officials responsible for the existing absentee system. The request concerns prospective eligibility rather than reopening the completed count. Relief against the state rule does not eliminate the independently pleaded federal equality claim.

### Recurring ballot-designation restrictions and election mootness

#### Schrader v. Blackwell

**Docket or dockets:** No Supreme Court docket stated; reviewed judgment: 241 F.3d 783 (6th Cir. 2001).

**Decided:** December 10, 2001.

For Schrader v. Blackwell, December 10, 2001, concerning the recognition-linked official designation, see Election Law — Official party designations and recognition requirements.

##### Whether the completed election and restored party recognition end the controversy

**Holding:** Completion of the election and the party's later recognition do not end this challenge to a recognition requirement capable of recurring against the continuing party plaintiff in elections too short for full review. The Court reaches the ballot-designation dispute under the capable-of-repetition exception.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas's opinion on the threshold question, joined by all eight other Justices.

**Material application:** The continuing party plaintiff is subject to periodic recognition requirements and has lost and regained recognition; recurrence can evade complete review within an election cycle.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The earlier filing-deadline challenge is outside the accepted issue.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Timmons v. Twin Cities Area New Party supports review of a continuing nomination dispute; its distinct conditional-enactment setting is not imported.

#### Utah v. Evans, 536 U.S. 452

**Docket or dockets:** No. 01-714.
**Decided:** 2002-06-20.

For the June 20, 2002 holdings in No. 01-714 on statutory and constitutional permission for residual hot-deck imputation, see Election Law.

##### Completed transmission does not defeat redress of the representation injury

**Holding:** A State has standing to contest a census method that causes an identified loss of a House seat when authoritative relief concerning the responsible census determination would likely correct the allocation through the statutory sequence. Completed transmission and certification do not categorically bar correction of court-determined legal error in the count.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Breyer's opinion of the Court is adopted by all nine Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Likely redress rests on correction through the enacted allocation sequence, not a coercive presidential injunction or a general presumption that absent persons obey judicial advice. The President's APA status and susceptibility to coercive relief remain reserved; Utah need not prevail on illegality to establish standing.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Franklin v. Massachusetts: applied to likely redress through the allocation sequence. Wisconsin v. City of New York: applied to continuing representational injury. Department of Commerce v. United States House of Representatives: applied to plaintiff-specific injury rather than statutory authorization alone.

#### Republican Party of Minnesota v. White, 536 U.S. 765

**Docket or dockets:** No. 01-521.
**Decided:** June 27, 2002.

##### A continuing speech constraint presents a prospective controversy without completed discipline

**Holding:** The continuing announce-clause restriction on the plaintiffs’ intended judicial-campaign speech presents a prospective controversy; completed disciplinary action is unnecessary.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of the opinion of the Court (Scalia), joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Kennedy and Thomas.

**Material application:** Wersal’s earlier complaint had been dismissed, but the continuing restraint and intended speech supported declaratory and prospective injunctive relief. No completed sanction is reviewed.

The content-restriction holding and confined prospective remedy in Republican Party of Minnesota v. White, No. 01-521, decided June 27, 2002, appear under First Amendment.

## Holdings volume: federal-courts-immunities-and-remedies

# SUPREME COURT OF THE UNITED STATES: HOLDINGS REGISTER

**Last completed October Term:** 2001
**Processed through:** June 28, 2002, after all 127 completed OT2001 Court events.
**Edition:** November 11, 2026

## Federal Courts: Immunities and Remedies

### Federal sovereign immunity

#### United States v. Nordic Village, Inc., 503 U.S. 30 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1629.
**Decided:** February 25, 1992.

##### Whether original Bankruptcy Code §106(c) waives immunity from this affirmative personal money judgment

**Holding:** Original Bankruptcy Code §106(c) does not unequivocally waive the United States’ immunity from an affirmative personal money judgment recovering an unauthorized transfer under §550. A plausible construction permitting binding determinations of governmental rights and estate liabilities without an affirmative payment order defeats the required clear consent to this monetary remedy.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia’s opinion, joined by Stone, White, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; seven Justices control the statutory threshold.

**Current force:** Remains controlling as stated for original §106(c) and the personal monetary remedy adjudicated.

**Material application:** The trustee avoided an unauthorized transfer under §549 and sought $20,000 from the United States under §550 after corporate money paid an officer’s individual taxes. Ordinary recovery authority under §550 does not answer the separate sovereign-consent question; no claim-linked waiver under §106(a) or (b) supplies this judgment.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The holding does not validate the diversion, strip §106(c) of effect, decide state constitutional immunity, or construe a different or later waiver. It does not alter §106(a) or (b); neither subsection supplies consent to the actual judgment.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Sixth Circuit’s judgment is reversed and the $20,000 personal money judgment set aside for want of sovereign consent. No substitute award is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Irwin v. Department of Veterans Affairs*, 498 U.S. 89; *United States v. Mitchell*, 445 U.S. 535; and *United States v. King*, 395 U.S. 1, supply the requirement of unequivocal consent to the particular monetary remedy. *Hoffman v. Connecticut Department of Income Maintenance*, 492 U.S. 96, supplies plurality statutory reasoning adopted for this federal question; its fractured state judgment is not a controlling federal-waiver holding. *Ruckelshaus v. Sierra Club*, 463 U.S. 680, and *McMahon v. United States*, 342 U.S. 25, prevent enlarging consent beyond Congress’s language. *United States v. Menasche*, 348 U.S. 528, supports giving both paragraphs of §106(c) operative work. *Dellmuth v. Muth*, 491 U.S. 223, supplies textual clear-statement reasoning without a new ruling about States.

##### Whether bankruptcy jurisdiction, property and trust characterizations, or equitable recoupment sustain this money judgment

**Holding:** A general grant of jurisdiction over bankruptcy-estate property in §1334(d), an asserted trust interest, or the origin of transferred money in estate assets does not supply federal consent to this personal money judgment. Because the trustee seeks a sum rather than identified property under the court’s control, this order cannot be sustained as a genuine property adjudication or as equitable recoupment.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia’s opinion, joined by Stone, White, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; the same seven Justices reject each alternative offered to sustain the monetary judgment.

**Material application:** The order imposes a general obligation to pay $20,000. No identified property or fund supports it, and the relief is an affirmative recovery rather than defensive recoupment.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A genuine specific-property proceeding requires its own facts and authority and is not adjudicated here. No traceable fund is assumed.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The alternative grounds do not preserve the personal money judgment. Genuinely distinct, properly supported remedies remain for ordinary proceedings; none is awarded.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Blatchford v. Native Village of Noatak*, 501 U.S. 775, distinguishes subject-matter jurisdiction from immunity. *United States v. Whiting Pools, Inc.*, 462 U.S. 198, is distinguished because tangible debtor-property turnover does not authorize this Treasury payment. *Begier v. IRS*, 496 U.S. 53, does not supply an identified fund here. *United States v. Shaw*, 309 U.S. 495, is preserved against inferring monetary consent from a property characterization. *Bull v. United States*, 295 U.S. 247, and *United States v. Dalm*, 494 U.S. 596, preserve recoupment’s limited defensive operation, which does not sustain this affirmative award.

#### United States Department of Energy v. Ohio, 503 U.S. 607 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 90-1341 and 90-1517.
**Decided:** April 21, 1992.

##### Whether the Clean Water Act and Resource Conservation and Recovery Act citizen-suit provisions waive immunity from the requested punitive fines

**Holding:** The CWA and RCRA citizen-suit provisions authorize proceedings against the United States but do not unequivocally expose it to the punitive civil penalties incorporated from their separate enforcement provisions. Those incorporations include the limits of the penalty provisions, whose generally defined liable persons exclude the United States.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter, Part II-A, joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas; six votes control. White, Blackmun and Stevens dissent on this issue.

**Material application:** The express inclusion of the United States among defendants does not alter the separate incorporated penalty provisions, whose general definitions of liable persons omit the United States.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The ruling concerns the provisions effective April 21, 1992 and the requested punitive fines. It decides no later amendment, other express waiver, particular coercive contempt sanction, settled pollution allegation, independent cause of action or fine amount. The stayed technical-study claim is unaffected.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The consolidated judgment is reversed and remanded. In No. 90-1341, the CWA facilities and RCRA citizen-suit punitive-waiver rulings are reversed, 6–3. In No. 90-1517, rejection of RCRA facilities waiver is sustained, 9–0, and the CWA citizen-suit alternative is rejected, 6–3. The contingent amount stipulation supplies no independent liability and operates only if a lawful remaining basis warrants it. Clear compliance duties and coercive relief remain distinct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States v. Mitchell, 445 U.S. 535 — applied: federal consent to the asserted liability must be unequivocal.
- United States v. Nordic Village, Inc., decision of February 25, 1992 — applied: a plausible narrower construction defeats asserted consent to the particular affirmative monetary remedy.
- Engel v. Davenport, 271 U.S. 33 — applied: statutory incorporation includes the referenced provision’s limitations.
- Ruckelshaus v. Sierra Club, 463 U.S. 680, and McMahon v. United States, 342 U.S. 25 — applied: waiver is not enlarged beyond what the enacted language requires.

##### Whether the Clean Water Act federal-facilities process-and-sanctions language waives immunity from past-violation punishment

**Holding:** The CWA federal-facilities provision’s submission to requirements, process and sanctions does not unequivocally waive immunity from the punitive fines sought for completed violations. In its statutory context, sanctions directed to enforcing process support coercive compliance relief without clearly adding retrospective punishment.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter, Part II-B.1, joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas; six votes control. White, Blackmun and Stevens dissent.

**Material application:** The statute twice links sanctions to process. That context permits the narrower reading of coercive measures securing compliance rather than punishment for completed substantive violations.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The ruling concerns the provisions effective April 21, 1992 and the requested punitive fines. It decides no later amendment, other express waiver, particular coercive contempt sanction, settled pollution allegation, independent cause of action or fine amount. The stayed technical-study claim is unaffected.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The consolidated judgment is reversed and remanded. In No. 90-1341, the CWA facilities and RCRA citizen-suit punitive-waiver rulings are reversed, 6–3. In No. 90-1517, rejection of RCRA facilities waiver is sustained, 9–0, and the CWA citizen-suit alternative is rejected, 6–3. The contingent amount stipulation supplies no independent liability and operates only if a lawful remaining basis warrants it. Clear compliance duties and coercive relief remain distinct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Penfield Co. of California v. SEC, 330 U.S. 585 — applied to distinguish coercive compliance sanctions from punishment.
- Hicks v. Feiock, 485 U.S. 624 — applied to the conditional character of coercive sanctions, without deciding a contempt case here.
- Shell Oil Co. v. Iowa Department of Revenue, 488 U.S. 19 — applied: statutory context determines the relevant meaning.
- United States v. Mitchell, 445 U.S. 535, and United States v. Nordic Village, Inc., February 25, 1992 — applied: ambiguity about the particular punitive remedy defeats the claimed waiver.

##### Whether EPA approval makes state penalties arise under federal law for the Clean Water Act waiver

**Holding:** State-law punitive penalties do not arise under federal law for the CWA federal-facilities waiver merely because EPA approved the state pollution program. The provision’s qualified reference to civil penalties, read with its coercive-enforcement language and the separate penalty definitions, does not unequivocally authorize the requested punitive fines under either asserted source.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter, Part II-B.2, joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas; six votes control. White, Blackmun and Stevens dissent.

**Material application:** Federal approval does not transform Ohio’s penalty statute into a federal source of liability. The reference to civil penalties does not independently enlarge the incorporated federal penalty definition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The ruling concerns the provisions effective April 21, 1992 and the requested punitive fines. It decides no later amendment, other express waiver, particular coercive contempt sanction, settled pollution allegation, independent cause of action or fine amount. The stayed technical-study claim is unaffected.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The consolidated judgment is reversed and remanded. In No. 90-1341, the CWA facilities and RCRA citizen-suit punitive-waiver rulings are reversed, 6–3. In No. 90-1517, rejection of RCRA facilities waiver is sustained, 9–0, and the CWA citizen-suit alternative is rejected, 6–3. The contingent amount stipulation supplies no independent liability and operates only if a lawful remaining basis warrants it. Clear compliance duties and coercive relief remain distinct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Gully v. First National Bank in Meridian, 299 U.S. 109 — applied by statutory analogy: federal permission does not make a state-law claim arise under federal law.
- International Bridge Co. v. New York, 254 U.S. 126 — applied: congressional approval does not itself create the state-law right.
- ICC v. Brotherhood of Locomotive Engineers, 482 U.S. 270 — applied: familiar statutory language may carry its established construction into another statute.
- Arkansas v. Oklahoma, decision of February 26, 1992 — distinguished: federal character of permit administration does not determine punitive-waiver scope or convert every state penalty into federal law.
- United States v. Mitchell, 445 U.S. 535, and United States v. Nordic Village, Inc., February 25, 1992 — applied: uncertain implications do not expand a clearer, narrower consent.

##### Whether the Resource Conservation and Recovery Act federal-facilities provision waives immunity from punitive fines

**Holding:** RCRA’s federal-facilities provision does not unequivocally waive federal immunity from punitive fines for past violations. Its submission to substantive and procedural requirements, including permits, reporting, injunctive relief and sanctions enforcing that relief, reaches compliance mechanisms without clearly authorizing retrospective penalties.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter, Part II-C, joined by the other eight Justices; all nine Justices join this holding.

**Material application:** Permitting, reporting, injunctions and sanctions enforcing injunctions address compliance; the text identifies no unequivocal mechanism for punishing completed violations.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The ruling concerns the provisions effective April 21, 1992 and the requested punitive fines. It decides no later amendment, other express waiver, particular coercive contempt sanction, settled pollution allegation, independent cause of action or fine amount. The stayed technical-study claim is unaffected.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The consolidated judgment is reversed and remanded. In No. 90-1341, the CWA facilities and RCRA citizen-suit punitive-waiver rulings are reversed, 6–3. In No. 90-1517, rejection of RCRA facilities waiver is sustained, 9–0, and the CWA citizen-suit alternative is rejected, 6–3. The contingent amount stipulation supplies no independent liability and operates only if a lawful remaining basis warrants it. Clear compliance duties and coercive relief remain distinct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Mitzelfelt v. Department of Air Force, 903 F.2d 1293 — adopted on this point: requirements can encompass standards and their implementation without punitive measures.
- Hancock v. Train, 426 U.S. 167 — distinguished: subjection to permitting procedures does not determine the scope of punitive remedies; amended statutory language receives its enacted effect.
- EPA v. California, 426 U.S. 200 — retained as pre-amendment statutory context, not authority to disregard the later express compliance duties.
- United States v. Mitchell, 445 U.S. 535 — applied: no unequivocal consent to punitive liability appears in the specified injunctive-enforcement language.

#### Lane v. Peña, 518 U.S. 187 (1996)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-365.
**Decided:** June 20, 1996.

##### Whether the Rehabilitation Act waives compensatory-damages immunity for the federally conducted academy program

**Holding:** Sections 504(a) and 505 of the Rehabilitation Act, read with §1003 of the Rehabilitation Act Amendments of 1986, do not unequivocally waive the United States’ immunity from Lane’s compensatory-damages claim arising from this federally conducted academy program. The substantive nondiscrimination duty does not itself supply consent to the particular Treasury remedy.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg; seven Justices.

**Material application:** Section 505(a)(2)’s Title VI remedies cover recipients or federal providers of financial assistance; that formulation does not unequivocally reach every federal program operator. Section 505(a)(1)’s Title VII channel concerns §501 federal employment. Section 505(b) permits reasonable attorney’s fees as costs, in the court’s discretion, to a prevailing party other than the United States. Section 1003 removes specified State immunity; its public-entity comparison can reach nonfederal recipients and supplies no independent general federal damages consent.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No differently situated federal assistance-provider claim, fee application, officer action or implied damages cause is decided. Franklin’s intentional-discrimination, recipient-responsibility, causation and compensable-loss conditions are not decided because waiver fails.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Damages rejection affirmed, 7–2; the discrimination determination and unchallenged reinstatement remain undisturbed. No damages calculation or new trial.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **United States v. Nordic Village, Inc., 503 U.S. 30 (1992):** Applies the requirement of unequivocal enacted consent to the particular affirmative monetary remedy; a plausible narrower statutory operation defeats the asserted waiver. The original §106(c) holding and its limits remain unchanged.
- **United States Department of Energy v. Ohio, 503 U.S. 607 (1992):** Applies the distinction between federal substantive or adjudicatory coverage and the limits of incorporated monetary-remedy provisions; no pollution-remedy question is reopened.
- **Franklin v. Gwinnett County Public Schools, 503 U.S. 60 (1992):** Preserves appropriate relief in the established Title IX action and distinguishes that remedial inquiry from federal sovereign consent. Compensation still requires proved intentional discrimination, recipient responsibility, causation and compensable loss; their satisfaction is not decided here.
- **FDIC v. Meyer, 510 U.S. 471 (1994):** Preserves the separate inquiries into waiver, substantive cause and recovery, including the effect of a genuinely broad sue-and-be-sued clause. No such clause supplies this academy claim's waiver; no agency Bivens question is decided.

### Foreign sovereign immunity

#### Republic of Argentina v. Weltover, Inc., 504 U.S. 607 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-763.
**Decided:** June 12, 1992.

##### Whether negotiable sovereign debt issued and rescheduled for a monetary program is commercial activity under the FSIA

**Holding:** Under 28 U.S.C. §§1603(d) and 1605(a)(2), an activity is commercial when its nature is the kind of conduct a private participant in a market can undertake; the sovereign's public purpose does not control that characterization. Argentina and Banco Central acted commercially in issuing and rescheduling these negotiable, dollar-denominated debt instruments, even though the instruments served a foreign-exchange program and refinanced existing obligations.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; eight Justices adopt this proposition. Stone concurs separately in the judgment without joining the opinion.

**Material application:** Negotiable dollar repayment promises bearing market-linked interest are means private borrowers can use. The foreign-exchange objective and refinancing of existing debt concern purpose and do not change the instruments' commercial nature.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The ruling concerns these instruments and this rescheduling. It does not make all public borrowing, currency regulation, expropriation or sovereign activity affecting money commercial. The second-clause domestic-act theory is not decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The denial of FSIA dismissal is affirmed; ordinary contract proceedings continue. No contract liability, damages, attachment or execution is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Verlinden B. V. v. Central Bank of Nigeria, 461 U.S. 480 (1983): applied for the restrictive-immunity framework and statutory exception channel; no additional citizenship or commercial-purpose restriction is derived from it.

##### Whether failure to make the contractually selected New York payment creates a direct effect in the United States

**Holding:** For the third clause of §1605(a)(2), an effect is direct when it follows as an immediate consequence of the defendant's activity; the statute adds no separate substantiality or foreseeability requirement, although wholly trivial effects remain outside the ordinary reach of the exception. Failure to make payment in New York, after the holders validly exercised the instruments' contractual payment option, produces the required direct effect in the United States even though the holders are foreign and their ultimate economic loss is abroad.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; eight Justices adopt this proposition. Stone concurs separately in the judgment without joining the opinion.

**Material application:** The holders validly selected New York under the instruments, and previous payments credited their accounts there. The promised dollars failed to arrive at the agreed place of performance.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Wholly trivial effects remain outside the exception's ordinary reach. New York's financial-center reputation is too remote to establish the required effect. Other payment arrangements and merely incidental domestic financial connections remain open; foreign plaintiffs' ultimate losses abroad do not negate this agreed domestic performance obligation.

**Operative remedy or transition:** This domestic nonpayment satisfies the third clause of §1605(a)(2), sustaining the denial of immunity-based dismissal without deciding contract merits or asset immunity.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Verlinden B. V. v. Central Bank of Nigeria, 461 U.S. 480 (1983): applied to permit jurisdiction over qualifying foreign-plaintiff actions; its general jurisdictional discussion is not a substantiality or foreseeability test for this clause.
- The Second Circuit's immediate-consequence formulation in the judgment below is sustained; its additional substantiality/foreseeability formulation is rejected, and its financial-center-reputation rationale supplies no controlling ground.

##### Whether the assumed constitutional-contacts objection requires a narrower reading of the direct-effect exception

**Holding:** Assuming solely for Argentina's interpretive objection that a foreign state receives the relevant constitutional protection, Argentina's purposeful dollar debt and offered, selected and previously used New York payment arrangements provide sufficient transaction-related contacts to defeat the asserted constitutional doubt. Constitutional avoidance therefore does not justify narrowing the statutory direct-effect clause in this case; the Court decides neither foreign-sovereign constitutional personhood nor an independent constitutional personal-jurisdiction claim.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; eight Justices adopt this proposition. Stone concurs separately in the judgment without joining the opinion.

**Material application:** Argentina purposefully offered New York payment in dollar instruments, honored that selection before the dispute and then failed to perform there; the connection is more than foreseeable routing through an American bank.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Foreign-state constitutional personhood is assumed solely to answer the interpretive contention and remains undecided. No independent constitutional personal-jurisdiction claim, jurisdiction over unrelated sovereign conduct, or alternative subject-matter jurisdiction ground is adjudicated.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The constitutional-avoidance objection supplies no ground to narrow the statutory exception or dismiss this action; undecided contract defenses and asset-immunity questions remain available.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- International Shoe Co. v. Washington, 326 U.S. 310 (1945): applied on an express assumption of constitutional protection, for purposeful contacts related to this dispute; no holding extends constitutional personhood to foreign states.

### Prospective equitable relief

#### Rufo v. Inmates of Suffolk County Jail; Rapone v. Inmates of Suffolk County Jail, 502 U.S. 367 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 90-954 and 90-1004.
**Decided:** January 15, 1992.

**Companion or consolidated matters:** Rufo and Rapone are consolidated petitions presenting the same modification request; both judgments are vacated.

##### What change justifies modification of an institutional consent decree under Rule 60(b)(5)

**Holding:** Under Rule 60(b)(5), the party seeking to modify an institutional consent decree must prove a significant change in fact or law that makes continued enforcement inequitable; an unforeseeable catastrophe is not required. Actual anticipation and acceptance of the relevant risk impose a heavy burden to justify relief, and dissatisfaction, a new administration, or the availability of a cheaper constitutional alternative alone does not suffice.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; five of eight participating Justices support the exact proposition. O'Connor concurs in the judgment without joining this rationale; Thomas did not participate.

**Current-force modification:** The Rule 60(b)(5) holding survives, but PLRA §802 supplies independent routes for federal-law civil prison-conditions relief, excluding habeas challenges to the fact or duration of confinement and expressly including prospective relief granted before enactment. Under §3626(b)(1), relief is terminable on a party's or intervener's motion two years after grant or approval, one year after denial of termination under that paragraph, or, for an order issued on or before enactment, two years after enactment. Section 3626(b)(2) entitles a defendant or intervener to immediate termination of prospective relief granted without findings that it is narrowly drawn, no broader than necessary to correct the federal violation and the least intrusive necessary means. The original §3626(b)(3) prevents termination when record-based written findings establish that relief remains necessary to correct a current or ongoing federal violation, is no broader than necessary, and is narrowly drawn and least intrusive. This current-or-ongoing formulation is preserved as enacted. Under original §3626(e)(2), relief subject to a pending motion is automatically stayed from day 30 for a (b)(1) or (b)(2) motion, or day 180 for a motion under other law, until final ruling. Admission establishes no motion, missing finding, termination or elapsed stay period; relief entered by a state court solely on state-law claims is outside §3626's limitations. The former equitable rule cannot alone foreclose these independent statutory avenues.

**Material application:** Officials reaffirmed the single-occupancy obligation in 1985 after the original 1979 decree. The centrality of that term and acceptance of known risks matter, but the lower court must assess inequity without treating unforeseeability as an absolute condition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Population growth, expense, and the need for the proposed design are asserted grounds, not Supreme Court findings satisfying the burden. Modification under Rule 60(b)(5) concerns the continuing prospective operation of an existing institutional decree following a demonstrated significant change in fact or law. It does not establish a legislative power to compel reinstatement of a completed private damages or limitations judgment, nor does it decide whether a separate valid statutory remedy may provide collateral relief from such a judgment.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Both First Circuit judgments are vacated and the cases remanded for the district court to determine whether proven changed circumstances make continued enforcement inequitable and whether the proposed modification responds suitably to those changes. The existing decree remains operative until lawfully modified.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *United States v. Swift & Co.*, 286 U.S. 106 — its disposition is preserved; its demanding factual formulation is confined to its setting rather than imposed universally.
- *System Federation No. 91 v. Wright*, 364 U.S. 642 — applied to preserve equitable power to adjust continuing obligations to changed conditions.
- *United States v. United Shoe Machinery Corp.*, 391 U.S. 244 — applied to connect modification to the continuing decree's purpose and operation.
- *Board of Education v. Dowell*, 498 U.S. 237 — reinforces contextual treatment of institutional equitable relief; no school-desegregation termination rule is imported.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Prison Litigation Reform Act of 1995, Pub. L. 104-134, Title VIII, enacted April 26, 1996: §802, independent §3626(b)(1)–(2) termination, (b)(3) limitation, (d) state-law exception, original (e)(2) stay and §802(b)(1) enactment transition. This is a noncase-law change, not an overruling or new judicial holding.

##### What fit and protections a justified consent-decree modification must preserve

**Holding:** A justified modification must be suitably tailored to the proven change and preserve lawful protection of the decree's remaining purposes; the court may not simply rewrite a consent decree to the constitutional minimum. Bell's clarification of constitutional limits does not by itself eliminate a voluntarily accepted single-cell obligation, and financial pressure cannot authorize unconstitutional confinement.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; five of eight participating Justices support the exact proposition. O'Connor concurs in the judgment without joining this rationale; Thomas did not participate.

**Current-force modification:** Rufo's equitable tailoring rule remains controlling where its premise is otherwise lawful. For covered prison-conditions relief, PLRA §802 additionally requires findings before entry or approval of a consent decree that the prospective relief is narrowly drawn, no broader than necessary to correct the particular plaintiff's federal violation and the least intrusive necessary means, with substantial weight to adverse public-safety and criminal-justice effects. Relief requiring or permitting officials to exceed state or local authority, or otherwise violating state or local law, additionally requires that federal law permit that contrary relief, that it be necessary to correct the federal violation and that no other remedy correct the violation. The original current-or-ongoing-violation limitation on statutory termination appears in the preceding question. A consensual obligation exceeding the constitutional minimum does not by itself defeat these statutory conditions. Private settlements not judicially enforceable beyond reinstatement of the settled proceeding remain distinct, and their breach may be pursued through available state-law remedies; relief entered by a state court solely under state law retains §3626(d)'s exception. No decree is dissolved by this source admission.

**Material application:** The district court may consider practical administration and cost in selecting lawful relief, while exercising its own equitable judgment; these considerations cannot replace proof of a qualifying change. Bell does not dissolve the officials' independently accepted single-cell promise.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court approves no double-cell design or number of cells and makes no determination that the proposed arrangement is constitutional or that remaining safeguards are adequate.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Local No. 93, International Association of Firefighters v. Cleveland*, 478 U.S. 501 — consent obligations may exceed what constitutional liability alone would require.
- *Bell v. Wolfish*, 441 U.S. 520 — its constitutional treatment of double-celling does not erase a consensual single-cell requirement.
- *System Federation No. 91 v. Wright*, 364 U.S. 642 — preserves adjustment for changed circumstances while maintaining judicial control of lawful relief.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Prison Litigation Reform Act of 1995, Pub. L. 104-134, Title VIII, enacted April 26, 1996: §802, consent-decree limits, private-settlement distinction and surviving equitable rule. This is a noncase-law change, not an overruling or new judicial holding.

#### Morales v. Trans World Airlines, Inc., 504 U.S. 374 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1604.
**Decided:** June 1, 1992.

For the direct-reference and independently sufficient fare-marketing-effects grounds of preemption in *Morales v. Trans World Airlines, Inc.*, June 1, 1992, No. 90-1604, see Constitutional Structure — Federal preemption.

##### Whether threatened fare-guideline enforcement permits federal prospective relief before the airlines expose themselves to repeated sanctions

**Holding:** Federal prospective relief may protect a party against imminent state enforcement forbidden by federal law when repeated serious sanctions make awaiting enforcement an inadequate practical remedy. Texas's announced enforcement of these fare guidelines presents that condition; preemption alone does not dispense with the required threatened injury.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas; five of eight participants. Stone states a compatible equitable ground separately without joining the opinion; Souter does not participate.

**Material application:** Texas formally announced intended suits. Continuing the advertisements would accumulate substantial liability, while suspending them during a test case and review would surrender the challenged conduct. Neither option provides an adequate practical substitute on this record.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Texas did not argue *Younger* abstention, which is not decided. A materially different threat still requires its own adequate-remedy and threatened-injury showing; no general exception for every preemption claim is created.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Retain declaratory and injunctive protection against the specified fare-guideline enforcement, subject to the separate scope limitation below.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Ex parte Young*, 209 U.S. 123, applies to imminent unlawful enforcement with repeated sanctions and no adequate practical test-case alternative. *O'Shea v. Littleton*, 414 U.S. 488, preserves adequate-remedy and threatened-injury requirements. *Younger v. Harris*, 401 U.S. 37, is not modified because abstention is not presented.

##### Whether the injunction may extend beyond the unlawful enforcement actually threatened

**Holding:** A preventive injunction against state enforcement must be confined to the unlawful enforcement actually threatened; repeating a federal preemption clause does not justify protection against hypothetical future applications. The decree's coverage of unthreatened airline advertising and operations exceeds the established controversy.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas; five Justices. All eight participants vote to remove the excess, but Stone, Blackmun and Stevens supply no additional joins to this rationale.

**Material application:** The enforcement notices concern fare advertising, not the entire range of airline operations covered by the decree. The decree would otherwise adjudicate state applications before the State asserted them.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Frequent-flyer programs, boarding compensation and other unthreatened practices are not adjudicated merely because the NAAG materials discuss them. Extension of relief to 33 other Attorneys General and the separately petitioned personal-jurisdiction dispute in No. 90-1606 are outside this review. No damages, immunity for other airline conduct or new territorial extension is ordered.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate the excess injunction and remand for narrowing to the specified fare-guideline controversy, retaining the declaratory and injunctive protection supported by the actual threat.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Public Service Commission of Utah v. Wycoff Co.*, 344 U.S. 237, excludes hypothetical regulatory disputes from preventive relief. *Ex parte Young*, 209 U.S. 123, supplies the imminent-enforcement boundary, and *O'Shea v. Littleton*, 414 U.S. 488, excludes conjectural injury.

#### Lee v. Weisman, 505 U.S. 577 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1014.
**Decided:** June 24, 1992.

For the Establishment Clause grounds in *Lee v. Weisman*, June 24, 1992, No. 90-1014, see First Amendment — Official school prayer.

##### Whether continued exposure at a forthcoming graduation preserves a live claim for prospective relief

**Holding:** A student who remains in the same public-school system and faces its challenged official prayer policy at her forthcoming high-school graduation has a live claim for prospective protection although her earlier graduation is over. Deborah Weisman's continued exposure permits review and preservation of the injunction without deciding her father's taxpayer standing.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Blackmun's opinion, joined by Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Souter; six Justices adopt the threshold proposition.

**Current force:** Remains controlling as stated for the student's continuing prospective exposure.

**Material application:** Deborah remains enrolled at Classical High School, and the stipulated record makes clergy-led prayer at her later graduation likely. Her relationship to that forthcoming policy application is concrete; formal optional attendance does not remove it.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court decides no taxpayer-standing rule and creates no general advisory-jurisdiction exception for completed school controversies that might recur.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Preserve review and the injunction against the challenged official practice. The injunction's substantive scope follows the First Amendment holding.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Engel v. Vitale, 370 U.S. 421, and School District of Abington Township v. Schempp, 374 U.S. 203, supply the substantive school-prayer interest without supplying a new taxpayer-standing rule.

#### Chamber of Commerce v. Reich

**Docket or dockets:** D.C. Circuit No. 95-5242; no Supreme Court docket assigned.
**Decided:** January 14, 1997.

For the separate questions in Chamber of Commerce v. Reich, D.C. Circuit No. 95-5242; no Supreme Court docket assigned decided January 14, 1997, see Labor and Employment — Proprietary public construction contracts (The procurement delegation does not authorize this labor-policy sanction).

##### Review of the implementing officer

**Holding:** A person injured by an officer's implementation of a restriction outside delegated statutory authority may seek prospective equitable restraint under federal-question jurisdiction unless Congress has displaced that remedy. Section 702 waives sovereign immunity for this nonmonetary official-capacity suit against the implementing Secretary even without a pleaded APA review cause, while preserving other legal and equitable review limits and relief prohibitions in another consent-to-suit statute; any injunction must identify the officer or successor responsible for compliance.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Kennedy), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Federal-question jurisdiction, equitable action and the immunity waiver perform different work; none creates damages or a claim for every statutory error. Presidential APA status, coercive relief against the President and an independent nondelegation theory remain undecided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirmed and remanded for prospective relief against the responsible Secretary or successor, confined to the unlawful replacement-based procurement restriction and the retained statutory and equitable limits. No damages, contract entitlement, reinstatement or employee-specific remedy follows.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** McAnnulty, Stark and Harmon support review of officers acting beyond statutory power. Larson and Dugan preserve the confined unauthorized-action distinction. Franklin v. Massachusetts permits review of completed subordinate acts without resolving presidential APA status. Dalton v. Specter is distinguished by the preclusion structure of its completed base-closure scheme.

#### Johnson v. Rodriguez

**Docket or dockets:** Fifth Circuit Nos. 95-50879 and 96-50382; no separate Supreme Court docket assigned.

**Decided:** 1998-02-17.

##### Standing, Board attribution and class relief

**Holding:** Johnson must establish his own redressable injury and basis for prospective relief. Systemwide relief for the challenged parole practice requires a proved ongoing official policy or widespread authorized practice, corresponding class injury and relief fitted to that violation; class certification and isolated employee references to litigation establish none of those predicates by themselves.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's Part IV-A, joined by all nine Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Common evidence may establish a common wrong, but the functional and geographic reach of relief must be supported. Class certification, isolated employee remarks and earlier findings against other agencies do not themselves establish Johnson’s injury or an attributable ongoing Board practice. This is no universal Monell prerequisite for every state-officer injunction.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The statewide injunction is not restored. Any relief must fit proved injury and attributable practice, respect valid security interests and applicable prospective-relief law, and leave the unappealed claims undisturbed.

##### Dependent fees

**Holding:** The attorney-fee award cannot rest on the reversed trial decree and remains vacated. Any later prevailing-party entitlement must follow the lawful judgment ultimately entered.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's Part IV-B, joined by all seven other Associates: eight direct joins. Stone-Zsela agrees in this disposition without an added ground.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The dependent fee award remains vacated; the remand supplies no present prevailing-party entitlement and forecloses neither lawful later fees nor their ordinary requirements.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Lewis supplies meaningful assistance, qualifying access injury and fitted common relief; its noncontrolling categorical secondary-assistance and initial-filing restrictions are not adopted.
- Lujan and Lyons preserve personal injury, causation and the prospective basis for relief.
- Monell's governmental-attribution principle is not converted into a universal condition for every state-officer injunction. No precedent is overruled.

For this case’s other questions, see Civil Rights (Initial parole and confidential-file process; Protest-letter equal protection; The separate writ-writer equality claim); First Amendment (Retaliation for protected litigation; Necessary assistance and independent petition activity).

### Scope of prospective relief for abortion requirements

#### Planned Parenthood of Southeastern Pennsylvania v. Casey, 505 U.S. 833 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 91-744 and 91-902.
**Decided:** June 29, 1992.

For the other questions in Planned Parenthood of Southeastern Pennsylvania v. Casey, June 29, 1992, Nos. 91-744 and 91-902, see Civil Rights — Reproductive liberty and constitutional precedent (retained protection, governing framework and timetable), Abortion emergency care, Abortion informed consent, Minor consent and independent authorization, Spousal control of reproductive choice, and Abortion reporting and public disclosure; Federal Courts — Scope of prospective relief for abortion requirements; and First Amendment — Abortion information and compelled speech. Each question has one primary entry.

##### No additional materials remand or interim injunction

**Holding:** The record does not sufficiently identify an unlawful state communication or compulsory distribution practice to justify the requested additional materials vacatur, focused remand or interim injunction. The facial dispositions stand at their stated scopes, while a concrete misleading or coercive implementation remains challengeable.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone's opinion, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 9 direct rationale joins including the author.

**Operative remedy or transition:** No additional materials vacatur, focused remand or interim injunction is ordered. Ordinary proceedings implement the actual component judgments; concrete misleading or coercive implementation remains challengeable.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Brockett v. Spokane Arcades, 472 U.S. 491 (1985): applied to require relief corresponding to the demonstrated defect while preserving lawful operation.

##### The parental-attendance objection and the denial of special relief

**Holding:** The Court denies an attendance-specific vacatur, remand or interim injunction, 9–0. Five Justices rest on the absence of an established enacted or authoritative compulsory-visit command. Four additionally would sustain the assumed operation with the available bypass. Only the first ground commands a majority.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone's opinion, joined by Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter: 5 direct rationale joins including the author. White, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas concur in the judgment on their additional assumed-operation ground.

**Material application:** District findings 232 and 238–259 describe anticipated attendance practices and burdens; Third Circuit footnote 23 reserves the statutory correctness of that interpretation. They do not establish an enacted or authoritative additional-visit command. A later actual duty requires its own burden, alternative and justification assessment; the existence of bypass is not an automatic cure.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Deny special attendance vacatur, remand and interim injunction. The four-Justice assumed-operation rationale does not become an independently sufficient Court holding.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Bellotti, Hodgson and Ohio v. Akron: preserve effective independent authorization at their actual scope.
- The general six-step standard: an additional legal obligation must first be established; no invented attendance condition is approved.

#### Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-1712.
**Decided:** February 13, 1995.

For the other questions in this decision, see Civil Rights — Abortion access and provision-specific review (The complete Casey standard governs the access and facial challenge; The abortion definition excludes an unintended diagnostic termination; The specified emergency terms supply a prospective clinical criterion; A doubtful default-fault rationale does not invalidate the general penalty by itself).

##### The access claims return for a supported assessment and prompt interim consideration

**Holding:** The access portion of the Eighth Circuit judgment is vacated, and that court must remand for the District Court to apply Casey to the challenged access duties on a properly supported record. The District Court must promptly consider the providers' request for interim protection under the applicable ordinary standard and current record; this Court neither reinstates the dissolved injunction automatically nor orders a final injunction, hearing or trial.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Part V on access remand and prompt interim consideration, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer: six Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Planned Parenthood of Southeastern Pennsylvania v. Casey, 505 U.S. 833 (1992): applies Step 6's matched relief and preserves its distinction between an actual duty and assumed administration; its case-specific denial of additional relief is no universal bar.
- Interstate Commerce Commission v. Transcon Lines, decided January 10, 1995: applies the distinction among remedial authority, violation and scope by analogy, without transferring its admitted public-enforcement violation or statutory injunction predicates.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Final statewide invalidation, the ultimate State justification, disputed factual burdens, a definitive state severance, actual criminal or civil culpability, damages, a compelled evidentiary hearing, and a new parental, speech or emergency-access claim are not decided. Affirmance of the specified vagueness grounds does not approve a materially different state construction or concrete enforcement.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Access review is vacated and remanded, 6–3; the abortion-definition, emergency-definition and associated limited general-penalty rejections are each affirmed, 8–1. The District Court must promptly consider interim protection under the ordinary standard and current record. No dissolved injunction is automatically restored, no final injunction or hearing is ordered, and no emergency-access claim unpressed below or new parental or speech claim is decided.

### State immunity and federal causes of action

#### Hilton v. South Carolina Public Railways Commission, 502 U.S. 197 (1991)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-848.
**Decided:** December 16, 1991.

##### Whether FELA liability of a state-owned interstate railroad remains enforceable in state court

**Holding:** The Federal Employers' Liability Act's established inclusion of state-owned interstate railroads remains enforceable in a competent state court. This rule does not revive constructive waiver of Eleventh Amendment immunity in federal court or establish a general rule for every Article I damages action against a State.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's opinion, joined by Stone, White, Stevens, and Souter; these five Justices supply a majority of the eight participants. Blackmun concurs in the judgment on a different ground and does not supply the controlling rationale.

**Material application:** The retained statutory construction governs the specialized railroad relationship. Congress retained the relevant statutory language, and South Carolina excluded FELA-covered employees from workers' compensation against that established background.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not reopen Hilton's dismissed federal suit, decide negligence or damages, or hold that entering commerce generally waives immunity.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The state-court judgment is reversed and the case remanded so the FELA claim may be adjudicated.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Parden's FELA coverage holding is reaffirmed as independent of its constructive federal-forum waiver rationale, which remains displaced by Welch. Welch expressly reserved the statutory coverage question. Will, construing a different statutory term, is distinguished. Howlett is applied: a competent state court may not use state immunity to defeat liability under valid federal law.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Alden v. Maine, No. 98-436, June 23, 1999: extends the state-court enforcement principle to the expressly authorized FLSA remedy through its own five-Justice constitutional holding; Hilton's retained FELA construction and specialized reliance grounds remain intact.

### Statutory attorney's fees

#### City of Burlington v. Dague, 505 U.S. 557 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-810.
**Decided:** June 24, 1992.

##### Whether contingency risk permits enhancement under the two environmental fee statutes

**Holding:** A reasonable attorney's fee under 42 U.S.C. §6972(e) and 33 U.S.C. §1365(d) may not be enhanced above a properly determined lodestar to compensate for the risk of nonpayment when compensation depends on success.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; five Justices adopt the categorical rule. The dissenters' market-based alternatives supply no controlling exception.

**Current force:** Remains controlling as stated under the two reviewed fee provisions; it is not a prohibition of every possible fee adjustment.

**Material application:** Reasonable hours and rates produced a $198,027.50 lodestar. The $49,506.87 contingency addition, reported as 25%, cannot stand even though the district court found substantial difficulty obtaining counsel without possible enhancement.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court uses reasonable-fee principles shared with similarly worded prevailing-party statutes but adjudicates this award under the two identified provisions. Differently worded compensation schemes, delay adjustments, unreasonable underlying rates and common-fund fees are not decided. Fee eligibility, hours and rates, expenses, state-law damages and the environmental injunction are outside the grant.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the judgment insofar as it sustains the $49,506.87 addition and remove that amount, preserving the $198,027.50 lodestar and unreviewed relief. No market-evidence remand or replacement multiplier is authorized.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Hensley v. Eckerhart, 461 U.S. 424, supplies the lodestar and avoidance of extensive collateral fee litigation. Blum v. Stenson, 465 U.S. 886, supplies the strong lodestar presumption, applicant's burden and nonduplication rule; its reserved contingency question is answered. Pennsylvania v. Delaware Valley Citizens' Council for Clean Air, 478 U.S. 546, supplies lodestar primacy without deciding delay or deficient rates. White's previously noncontrolling categorical position in Pennsylvania v. Delaware Valley Citizens' Council for Clean Air, 483 U.S. 711, is adopted for these statutes; the competing limited and broader permission theories remain noncontrolling. Blanchard v. Bergeron, 489 U.S. 87, establishes that the private contingent agreement does not determine the statutory fee. Ardestani v. INS, December 10, 1991, remains distinct on administrative fee eligibility; Department of Energy v. Ohio, April 21, 1992, supplies no federal-immunity premise for this municipal award.

#### National Private Truck Council, Inc. v. Oklahoma Tax Commission

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-688.
**Decided:** June 19, 1995.

For the other questions in National Private Truck Council, Inc. v. Oklahoma Tax Commission, decided June 19, 1995, No. 94-688. see Federal Courts — State-tax equitable relief (Federal tax equity and an actually adequate legal remedy).

##### Fees follow an available fee-eligible federal claim

**Holding:** Because the invoked §1983 equitable action affords no relief on this record, §1988 supplies no fee entitlement through that action. Success on state law does not categorically defeat fees where an independently viable fee-eligible federal claim exists.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court; all nine Justices join this proposition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Maher continues to permit fees through an available fee-eligible action without an express federal merits decision. No independently viable alternative fee-eligible federal claim is established here; reasonable amount, an unpresented damages action and the independent state fee ground remain outside review.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Maher v. Gagne: distinguishes an available fee-eligible action resolved without federal merits from this unavailable federal equitable action; its rule remains intact.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm denial of the reviewed section 1983 injunction/declaration and section 1988 fees while preserving the existing tax invalidity and adequate state-law refund entitlement. No tax sum, separate damages determination, independent state fee ruling or new federal remedial direction follows.

### Collateral appeals from immunity denials

#### Puerto Rico Aqueduct & Sewer Authority v. Metcalf & Eddy, Inc., 506 U.S. 139 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1010.

**Decided:** January 12, 1993.

##### Immediate appeal from a qualifying denial of Eleventh Amendment immunity

**Holding:** A State or entity claiming to be an arm of a State may immediately appeal under §1291 a conclusive denial of Eleventh Amendment immunity from federal suit when the immunity issue is separate from the merits and the protection would be effectively lost by waiting for final judgment. Appealability does not establish that the claimant possesses immunity, and a dispute over arm-of-State status does not by itself require postponing review until trial.

**Proposition-level authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Blackmun, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas: seven votes for this rule and its controlling explanation. Stone-Zsela supplies an eighth vote for reversal and the narrower procedural rule in his judgment concurrence. No fractured-decision analysis is necessary.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Cohen v. Beneficial Industrial Loan Corp., 337 U.S. 541 (1949), and Coopers & Lybrand v. Livesay, 437 U.S. 463 (1978): applied; the three collateral-order requirements remain mandatory.
- Mitchell v. Forsyth, 472 U.S. 511 (1985): its immunity-from-suit rationale is applied to asserted Eleventh Amendment immunity; this does not extend substantive immunity to new entities.
- Edelman v. Jordan, 415 U.S. 651 (1974), and In re Ayers, 123 U.S. 443 (1887): relied on for the existing protection from federal suit and its federalism basis, without enlarging the classes of immune defendants.
- Ex parte Young, 209 U.S. 123 (1908), Green v. Mansour, 474 U.S. 64 (1985), and Cory v. White, 457 U.S. 85 (1982): preserved as distinct rules concerning prospective official relief, past-violation declarations, and suits against the State or agency itself.
- Van Cauwenberghe v. Biard, 486 U.S. 517 (1988), and Catlin v. United States, 324 U.S. 229 (1945): distinguished; their ordinary forum-jurisdiction finality rules remain.
- Mt. Healthy City Board of Education v. Doyle, 429 U.S. 274 (1977): retained; entity status remains a distinct inquiry.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Entitlement to immunity, arm-of-State status, waiver, abrogation, Puerto Rico's unchallenged treatment as a State and contract remedies remain undecided. Ex parte Young's qualifying prospective official relief remains distinct from immunity of a State or agency itself and declarations of past violations.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the First Circuit appellate-jurisdiction dismissal and remand for the immunity inquiry; no declaration that PRASA is immune or dismissal of the contract action.

#### Johnson v. Jones

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-455.
**Decided:** June 12, 1995.

##### Factual sufficiency is not an immediate qualified-immunity appeal

**Holding:** A denial of qualified immunity is not immediately appealable under §1291 insofar as it determines only whether the pretrial evidence presents a genuine factual dispute about the official's participation in the alleged conduct. Mitchell's immediate appeal remains available for the separable legal question whether the conduct, on the relevant assumed facts, violated clearly established law; the present excessive-force participation dispute does not meet that condition.

**Proposition-level authority:** Justice Breyer's opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; all nine adopt this bounded rule.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A proper legal immunity appeal uses the district court's assumed facts. If unstated, the appellate court may identify likely assumptions favorable to the nonmovant without deciding truth or reweighing sufficiency. An abstract legal contention does not bootstrap factual review. Genuine necessary connected review and future mixed-order questions remain at their reserved scope.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Cohen v. Beneficial Industrial Loan Corp., 337 U.S. 541 (1949): applies separateness as a cumulative collateral-order condition.
- Mitchell v. Forsyth, 472 U.S. 511 (1985): retains immediate legal immunity review on assumed facts, bounded against ordinary factual-sufficiency appeals.
- Digital Equipment Corp. v. Desktop Direct (June 6, 1994): applies category and cumulative-condition discipline; public immunity importance does not erase separateness.
- Swint v. Chambers County Commission (March 1, 1995): applies rejection of convenience-based appellate expansion; genuine necessary connected review remains at its reserved scope.
- Elder v. Holloway (February 23, 1994): preserves review of all relevant date-eligible legal authority on an authorized immunity issue; supplies no fact-reweighing jurisdiction.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm dismissal of the reviewed immediate excessive-force factual-sufficiency appeal. The circuit's separate false-arrest immunity reversal remains undisturbed. No participation, beating, evidence-sufficiency, liability, state-law claim or other defendant's liability is adjudicated.

#### Kimberlin v. Quinlan

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-2068.
**Decided:** June 12, 1995.

For the other questions in Kimberlin v. Quinlan, decided June 12, 1995, No. 93-2068. see Civil Rights — Qualified immunity and motive evidence (Circumstantial proof remains available in motive-dependent claims).

##### Separable legal review of the categorical evidence rule

**Holding:** An interlocutory qualified-immunity appeal may resolve whether the law permits a categorical direct-evidence condition without deciding disputed motive, participation or causation. Review of that separable legal condition does not authorize appellate selection between contested factual accounts.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer; all nine support this legal-review proposition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Actual motive, credibility, participation, personal responsibility, causation, security justification and episode-specific immunity remain for lawful consideration; appellate legal review selects no contested factual account.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Mitchell v. Forsyth, 472 U.S. 511 — applied to separable legal immunity review, without authorizing appellate factfinding.
- Elder v. Holloway — applied to independent review of contemporaneous legal authority on the proper record; no ultimate immunity ruling follows.
- Digital Equipment Corp. v. Desktop Direct, Inc. — applied to preserve the requirement of legal separability and finality.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate the direct-evidence-dependent First Amendment summary-judgment directive and remand without that categorical barrier. Independently sustainable episode-specific judgments and lawful discovery limits remain available. No trial, unlimited discovery, retaliation finding or damages award is ordered. The Fifth Amendment dismissal and Title III matters remain undisturbed.

#### Clanton v. Cooper

**Docket or dockets:** Tenth Circuit No. 96-7082; no separate Supreme Court docket stated.

**Decided:** 1997-12-29.

For warrant and detention deception, the separate confession branch and personal immunity and the case-wide mandate, see Clanton v. Cooper, Tenth Circuit No. 96-7082; no separate Supreme Court docket stated, decided 1997-12-29, Civil Rights — Material official deception and qualified immunity.

##### Immediate review separates legal immunity from historical facts

**Holding:** An interlocutory qualified-immunity appeal may resolve a separable legal defense on the proper facts assumed in the nonmovant's favor, but may not decide factual sufficiency, participation, credibility or which disputed account is true. Ultimate constitutional voluntariness is not wholly historical merely because its test considers circumstances.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part I, unanimous.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Johnson and Behrens permit review of a separable legal immunity defense on proper assumed facts and exclude evidentiary-sufficiency appeals. Miller distinguishes ultimate constitutional voluntariness from historical facts; Ornelas preserves task-specific review. Section 1254 supplies Supreme Court review without enlarging §1291 authority.

#### Danahy v. Buscaglia

**Docket or dockets:** Second Circuit No. 97-7264; no separate Supreme Court docket stated.

**Decided:** 1998-02-12.

For the particular November 1995 patronage-immunity application and the case-wide mandate, see Danahy v. Buscaglia, Second Circuit No. 97-7264; no separate Supreme Court docket stated, decided 1998-02-12, Civil Rights — Political affiliation and conduct-date immunity.

##### A legal immunity appeal accepts the proper factual assumptions

**Holding:** An interlocutory qualified-immunity appeal may resolve whether the properly assumed conduct violated clearly established law, but may not choose among disputed accounts of duties, knowledge, participation or motive. Section 1254 permits review of the circuit judgment without enlarging the circuit's §1291 jurisdiction; the particular notice question here is separable, and no additional factual-appeal component requiring dismissal has been identified.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stevens), Parts I–II, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Patronage motive and nonpolicymaker status are assumed; the Court selects no competing historical account. Ordinary confidentiality, exempt or at-will status, institutional importance and subjective innocence do not independently supply immunity.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Johnson, Behrens, Clanton and Smith permit the separable notice defense on proper assumed facts while forbidding selection among disputed duties, participation, knowledge or motive. Review of the circuit judgment does not enlarge its interlocutory authority.

#### Smith v. Mattox

**Docket or dockets:** Eleventh Circuit No. 96-6648; no separate Supreme Court docket stated.

**Decided:** 1998-01-12.

For arrest-force notice and the retained immunity framework and the case-wide mandate, see Smith v. Mattox, Eleventh Circuit No. 96-6648; no separate Supreme Court docket stated, decided 1998-01-12, Civil Rights — Arrest force and qualified immunity.

##### The separable legal immunity question is reviewable on the proper assumed account

**Holding:** The Court may decide whether the plaintiff-favorable conduct was clearly established as unlawful without resolving whether that conduct occurred. It may not select between accounts of compliance, continued resistance or gun-reaching, decide credibility or review evidentiary sufficiency.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part I, unanimous.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Johnson and Behrens preserve legal immunity review while excluding factual selection. Clanton confirms that disputed evidence does not bar a genuine legal question; §1254 does not enlarge circuit authority under §1291.

### Foreign sovereign commercial activity

#### Saudi Arabia v. Nelson, 507 U.S. 349 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-522.

**Decided:** March 23, 1993.

##### The first commercial-activity clause requires the action to rest on that activity

**Holding:** Under the first clause of 28 U.S.C. §1605(a)(2), commercial activity carried on in the United States—activity having substantial United States contact under §1603(e)—must supply the basis of the action, rather than merely precede the injury or establish a factual connection with it.
