The inquiry identifies the conduct constituting the elements that would entitle the plaintiff to relief; recruitment, a United States employment contract and a commercial retaliatory motive do not by themselves make the intentional detention-and-torture claims an action based on domestic commercial activity.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's opinion for the Court, excluding its warning paragraph, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas: six. White and Blackmun agree in the intentional-claim judgment on a separate domestic-nexus ground.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Republic of Argentina v. Weltover, June 12, 1992, decision: applies the nature-of-conduct rule; distinguishes its third-clause direct-effect holding from the first-clause basis requirement.

##### Alleged abuse of sovereign police power is not commercial conduct

**Holding:** Arrest, imprisonment and torture alleged to have been inflicted through a foreign state's police authority are sovereign in nature for §1605(a)(2), even if undertaken to retaliate against a commercial employee's safety complaints. The asserted commercial-activity exception therefore does not support jurisdiction over these intentional-tort claims; this statutory immunity ruling neither legalizes the abuse nor decides an unasserted exception or liability of absent individual actors.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Court opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas: six. Intentional-claim reversal receives eight judgment votes; White and Blackmun reach it without joining the sovereign-nature characterization.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Republic of Argentina v. Weltover, June 12, 1992, decision: applies nature rather than purpose; its market-participant distinction remains controlling.

##### Warning claims and absence of a controlling rationale

**Authority of the judgment:** The warning claims are dismissed under the asserted commercial-activity exception, 5–4: Souter, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas and White support reversal; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens and Kennedy oppose that component. Souter’s four-Justice warning paragraph rejects relabeling the sovereign wrong. White instead treats retaliatory employment conduct as commercial but finds actionable conduct abroad and an insufficient domestic nexus. Neither rationale is a logical subset of the other, so Marks supplies no controlling general warning rule.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The judgment binds these parties but does not establish that every independently pleaded domestic recruitment-warning omission is barred. The opposing writings establish no jurisdictional predicates or merits findings.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The first-clause basis inquiry is distinct from Weltover’s third-clause direct effects. No general rule requires every act in an action to be commercial, and no separate ruling on recruitment contacts is necessary. Other exceptions and absent individuals’ liability remain undecided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and require jurisdictional dismissal under the asserted exception: 8–1 for intentional claims and 5–4 for warning claims. No tort merits, duty, attribution, knowledge, causation, entity identity or preclusion determination follows.

### FTCA territorial scope

#### Smith v. United States (Antarctica), 507 U.S. 197 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1538.

**Decided:** March 8, 1993.

##### The FTCA does not authorize this Antarctic tort action

**Holding:** The foreign-country exception in § 2680(k), read with the FTCA's enacted liability and place-of-law provisions, excludes this claim based entirely on acts or omissions in Antarctica even though no other sovereign is recognized there. Sections 1346(b) and 2674 do not authorize substituting the claimant's Oregon law for the law of the Antarctic place of the alleged negligence merely because she resides in Oregon; her valid residence venue does not supply the missing substantive consent.

**Proposition-level authority:** The statutory portion of White's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas: eight Justices adopt this holding. Stevens dissents.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States v. Spelar, 338 U.S. 217 (1949): its territorial understanding of the exception is applied to Antarctica; its foreign-sovereignty facts do not fix an exclusive definition requiring another recognized sovereign.
- Richards v. United States, 369 U.S. 1 (1962): applied; the whole law of the place of the negligent act controls the statutory choice, and the claimant's residence does not authorize a different starting point.
- United States v. Kubrick, 444 U.S. 111 (1979): applied to respect the intended scope of the FTCA waiver without judicial expansion or contraction.
- Beattie v. United States, 756 F.2d 91 (D.C.Cir.1984): its conclusion permitting the Antarctic FTCA action is disapproved; the territorial/choice-of-law objection in Scalia's dissent is consistent with the adopted statutory construction.
- United States v. Nordic Village, February 25, 1992: preserved at its actual scope concerning clear consent to affirmative bankruptcy monetary relief; it does not independently decide this different express waiver.
- Molzof v. United States, January 14, 1992: preserved; its construction of punitive damages and independent local-law entitlement do not establish Antarctic territorial coverage.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The claim concerns acts wholly in Antarctica. No blanket rule forecloses every offshore FTCA, constitutional or independently authorized remedy. Extraterritoriality discussion is confirmatory, not an independently sufficient universal rule; maritime and other waivers remain distinct.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm dismissal under the foreign-country exception; no negligence determination or damages award.

### Federal sovereign immunity from monetary assessments

#### United States v. Idaho ex rel. Director, Idaho Department of Water Resources, 508 U.S. 1 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-190.
**Decided:** May 3, 1993.

##### Adjudicatory consent does not authorize these monetary assessments

**Holding:** The McCarran Amendment subjects the United States to the state procedures governing the water adjudication, but does not specifically waive immunity from Idaho’s challenged filing assessments. Its express exclusion of judgments for costs and the blurred line between these assessments and former costs reinforce that conclusion; participation in the adjudication does not itself create liability for the charges.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not classify every filing fee as costs or exempt the United States from ordinary adjudicatory procedure. A different express monetary waiver is not decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the fee-imposing judgment and remand; federal participation in the comprehensive water-rights adjudication continues. No allocation or reimbursement amount is adjudicated.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States v. District Court in and for Eagle County, 401 U.S. 520: comprehensive adjudicatory waiver preserved, not converted to universal payment consent.
- Library of Congress v. Shaw, 478 U.S. 310: applies specificity of monetary consent.
- United States v. Nordic Village, February 25, 1992: particular monetary-waiver principle applied by analogy; bankruptcy holding unchanged.

### Federal Tort Claims Act exhaustion

#### McNeil v. United States, 508 U.S. 106 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-6033.
**Decided:** May 17, 1993.

##### Administrative exhaustion must precede the original FTCA action

**Holding:** An FTCA claimant must present the claim to the appropriate federal agency and receive its final written denial, sent by certified or registered mail, before instituting the action; after six months without final disposition, the claimant may instead elect to treat the inaction as final denial. Exhaustion achieved after filing does not cure the original premature action, even when little litigation has occurred; §2675(a) expressly excepts claims asserted under the Federal Rules by third-party complaint, crossclaim or counterclaim.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Later papers as a possible new action and the limitations or other defenses to that action remain undecided. Liberal construction of uncounseled pleadings does not excuse the statutory sequence.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm dismissal of the original premature action; no tort liability, transfer or new-action timeliness is adjudicated.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Hallstrom v. Tillamook County, 493 U.S. 20: applied to enforce a congressionally fixed prefiling sequence.
- McCarthy v. Madigan, March 4, 1992: statutory primacy applied; its damages-only BOP exception is distinguished.
- Haines v. Kerner, 404 U.S. 519: liberal pleading construction preserved; it does not excuse the prefiling requirement.

### Concurrent claims against the United States

#### Keene Corp. v. United States, 508 U.S. 200 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-166.
**Decided:** May 24, 1993.

##### Section 1500 jurisdiction is measured when the action is filed

**Holding:** Section 1500 bars the reviewed Claims Court action when, at filing, a suit for or in respect to the same claim is pending elsewhere against the United States or a covered federal actor; later dismissal of that other suit does not cure the original action’s jurisdictional defect. Claims Court decisions inconsistent with that filing-time requirement cannot support jurisdiction here; the Court does not decide the reversed order of filing.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Mollan v. Torrance, 9 Wheat.537: filing-time jurisdiction applied.
- Corona Coal Co. v. United States, 263 U.S. 537: statutory pendency bar applied.
- Brown v. United States, 175 Ct.Cl. 343, 358 F.2d 1002, and Hossein v. United States, 218 Ct.Cl. 727: disapproved insofar as inconsistent with filing-time jurisdiction.
- Tecon Engineers, Inc. v. United States, 170 Ct.Cl. 389, 343 F.2d 943: reversed filing sequence reserved, not overruled here.

##### Overlapping facts and relief cannot be separated merely by changing legal theories

**Holding:** For the claims presented, §1500 applies when the suits arise from substantially the same operative facts and seek overlapping relief, even if they plead different legal theories or those theories cannot be brought in the same forum. The Court leaves open suits seeking wholly different relief and does not adopt the Federal Circuit’s broader disposition of those precedents.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- British American Tobacco Co. v. United States, 89 Ct.Cl.438: operative-facts construction adopted for overlapping relief.
- Matson Navigation Co. v. United States, 284 U.S. 352: historical statutory setting applied; different causes do not negate the bar.
- Casman v. United States, 135 Ct.Cl. 647, and Boston Five Cents Savings Bank v. United States, 864 F.2d 137: wholly different relief question reserved, not overruled here.

##### The dismissals do not depend on a new rule requiring prospective-only treatment

**Holding:** Keene’s reviewed suits fail under established filing-time and factual-identity principles; the Federal Circuit’s broader repudiation of precedents unnecessary to this result does not make the controlling rule new as applied here. The Court therefore rejects the requested prospective-only treatment without deciding the general availability of pure prospectivity.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Claims Court-first filing, wholly different relief, future-action tolling and general pure prospectivity remain unresolved. The holding does not adopt the Federal Circuit’s broader repudiation of those distinct precedents.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm jurisdictional dismissal of Keene I and Keene II; later termination elsewhere does not revive these actions. No asbestos merits, indemnity, compensation, transfer or new filing opportunity is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Chevron Oil Co. v. Huson, 404 U.S. 97: prospective-only request rejected at its new-rule premise; general availability not decided.
- Corona Coal Co. v. United States, 263 U.S. 537, and British American Tobacco Co. v. United States, 89 Ct.Cl. 438: existing governing principles applied.

### Social Security remands and EAJA fees

#### Shalala v. Schaefer, 509 U.S. 292 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-311.
**Decided:** June 24, 1993.

##### Sentence-four judgment ends the action and confers prevailing status

**Holding:** A sentence-four judgment reversing the Secretary’s denial of benefits with a remand ends the §405(g) civil action and makes the successful claimant a prevailing party; the court must enter judgment rather than retain the merits action pending an administrative award. Sentence-six remands instead retain jurisdiction for the statutorily specified proceedings and later return to court.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Sullivan v. Finkelstein, 496 U.S. 617, and Melkonyan v. Sullivan, 501 U.S. 89: the two statutory remand routes applied.
- Texas State Teachers Assn. v. Garland Independent School District, 489 U.S. 782, and Farrar, December 14, 1992: enforceable judicial relief establishes prevailing status, not automatic fee amount.

##### The missing separate judgment prevents the fee deadline from expiring

**Holding:** Under the then-effective rules, EAJA’s thirty-day application period follows a properly entered judgment and expiration of the applicable sixty-day appeal period; the absence of the required Rule 58 separate judgment prevents that deadline from expiring. Schaefer’s application is timely although the sentence-four remand was itself a final merits disposition.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States v. Indrelunas, 411 U.S. 216: separate-document requirement applied as then in force.
- Bankers Trust Co. v. Mallis, 435 U.S. 381: ability to take an appeal without the document distinguished from expiration of appellate time.
- Melkonyan, 501 U.S. 89: final court judgment, not agency decision, governs the EAJA clock.

##### Hudson does not preserve administrative-fee coverage after an ordinary terminated sentence-four action

**Holding:** Hudson’s integrated administrative-proceeding fee rationale does not apply to a sentence-four remand properly entered without retained jurisdiction, or to retention of jurisdiction that is challenged. It remains applicable to qualifying sentence-six proceedings in which the court retains the action, subject to EAJA’s other requirements.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; White, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Sentence six permits a pre-answer remand on the Secretary’s good-cause motion, or new material evidence with good cause for earlier omission, retaining jurisdiction for return and later judgment. Prevailing status does not establish every EAJA condition or amount. No later deemed-entry deadline is imported.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the timeliness determination and reviewed award; enter the required separate judgment where still absent. No new administrative-services award or reopening of unreviewed fee issues.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Sullivan v. Hudson, 490 U.S. 877: retained-jurisdiction rationale limited in light of the statutory distinction; sentence-six coverage preserved.
- Finkelstein, 496 U.S. 617, and Melkonyan, 501 U.S. 89: sentence-four termination controls; statutory retention is reserved for sentence six.

### Federal agency immunity and constitutional damages

#### FDIC v. Meyer, 510 U.S. 471

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-741.

**Decided:** 1994-02-23.

##### FTCA exclusivity does not encompass this federal constitutional claim

**Holding:** Section 2679(a) makes FTCA relief exclusive as against an agency's sue-and-be-sued authority only for claims cognizable under the whole of §1346(b), including its private-person and state-law requirements. Meyer's federal constitutional tort is outside that category, so §2679(a) does not bar this suit in the agency's own name; this statutory conclusion neither supplies a cause of action nor guarantees recovery.

**Proposition-level authority:** The statutory-exclusivity portion of Thomas's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg. All nine adopt this separate threshold holding.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The §1346(b) category requires a claim against the United States for money damages for property injury or loss, personal injury or death caused by an employee's negligent or wrongful act or omission within employment scope, under circumstances in which a private person would be liable under the law of the place. That last requirement means state substantive law; §2679(b)(1)'s differently worded employee rule is not imported.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Richards v. United States, 369 U.S. 1: applies the FTCA's state-law source and private-person limitation.
- Miree v. DeKalb County, 433 U.S. 25: applies state law as the substantive source under the law-of-the-place provision.
- Loeffler v. Frank, 486 U.S. 549: preserves FTCA coordination while specifying that §2679(a) refers to cognizability under §1346(b), not everything described as tortious.
- United States v. Smith, 499 U.S. 160: distinguishes employee exclusivity under §2679(b)(1) and its treatment of the foreign-country exception from the differently worded agency provision.
- Hubsch v. United States, 338 U.S. 440: preserves cognizability despite failure of proof on an element; distinguishes a claim that never falls within §1346(b)'s category.

##### Former FSLIC's sue-and-be-sued clause waives immunity

**Holding:** Former 12 U.S.C. §1725(c)(4) waives FSLIC's sovereign immunity for this constitutional tort claim, even though a private entity ordinarily would not bear that constitutional duty. An implied restriction on its broad sue-and-be-sued authority requires a clear showing that the suit is inconsistent with the statutory or constitutional scheme, that restriction is necessary to avoid grave interference with governmental functions, or that Congress plainly intended the clause in a narrow sense; FDIC establishes none here.

**Proposition-level authority:** The waiver portion of Thomas's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg. Nine Justices adopt the specific waiver and its stated limits.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- FHA v. Burr, 309 U.S. 242: applies liberal construction and all three grounds for a clearly established implied restriction; mere absence of private constitutional liability does not establish one.
- Loeffler v. Frank, 486 U.S. 549: applies the broad waiver rule while keeping separate statutory limits distinct.
- Franchise Tax Board v. United States Postal Service, 467 U.S. 512: preserves private-business liability as support for the incidents of suit recognized there, without turning it into an outer boundary for every waiver.
- United States v. Nordic Village, February 25, 1992: preserves the requirement to identify the particular sovereign consent; the ambiguous bankruptcy provision there does not narrow this express agency clause.

##### Waiver does not create a direct agency Bivens action

**Holding:** A waiver of sovereign immunity removes a defense to suit but does not itself authorize constitutional damages; Bivens does not supply Meyer a direct damages cause of action against FSLIC. Extending an individual-officer action to the federal agency would create a distinct defendant and public obligation, rather than merely provide relief within an existing agency action.

**Proposition-level authority:** The cause-of-action portion of Thomas's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg. All nine adopt this independently sufficient ground for rejecting the agency award.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States v. Mitchell, 463 U.S. 206: applies the analytical separation of sovereign consent and a substantive source authorizing relief.
- Bivens v. Six Unknown Named Agents, 403 U.S. 388: retains its individual-officer action and declines extension directly to a federal agency.
- Davis v. Passman, 442 U.S. 228: preserves the individual employment-discrimination action; it does not supply an agency defendant here.
- Carlson v. Green, 446 U.S. 14: preserves individual-officer liability and its deterrent rationale without making every useful compensatory arrangement an authorized agency action.
- Franklin v. Gwinnett County Public Schools, February 26, 1992: preserves appropriate relief within an established private action; distinguishes creation of a new action and defendant.

**Independent alternative holding:** Even assuming that a direct agency remedy could advance the compensatory and deterrent purposes of Bivens, the resulting expansion of direct federal financial liability and redesign of the remedial structure counsel against judicial creation of that remedy. Those institutional considerations independently require rejection of this implied agency damages action; the Court does not find that Meyer received an adequate substitute remedy.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** The independent fiscal-and-remedial portion of Thomas's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg. Nine Justices adopt this independently sufficient alternative ground.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The $130,000 agency award cannot stand for want of an agency cause. Pattullo's separate immunity judgment is outside review and remains undisturbed. The Court does not decide Meyer's property entitlement, the underlying due-process violation, entitlement under another source, a universal federal waiver, or elimination of existing individual Bivens remedies. Former §1725(c)(4), rather than a silently substituted successor clause, governs this 1982 agency claim. Section 2679(b)(1)'s separate employee rule is not rewritten.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Bivens v. Six Unknown Named Agents, 403 U.S. 388: applies its special-factors limitation to the proposed direct public liability, independently of the individual-defendant distinction.
- United States v. Standard Oil Co. of California, 332 U.S. 301: applies the concern against judicial determination of a new federal fiscal obligation.
- Bush v. Lucas, 462 U.S. 367: applies institutional restraint concerning a new damages remedy; does not find that Bush's civil-service scheme governed or compensated Meyer.
- Schweiker v. Chilicky, 487 U.S. 412: applies caution about remedy creation despite incomplete compensation, without importing its Social Security system as Meyer's actual remedy.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The agency damages judgment is reversed and the $130,000 award cannot stand for want of a direct agency cause. Pattullo's separate, unreviewed immunity judgment remains undisturbed. No constitutional merits finding or new damages trial is ordered.

### Speech injunctions and remedial scope

#### Madsen v. Women’s Health Center, Inc., 512 U.S. 753

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-880.

**Decided:** 1994-06-30.

For the other questions in Madsen v. Women’s Health Center, Inc., 1994-06-30, No. 93-880, see First Amendment — The decree as confined is not categorically content based; First Amendment — A neutral speech injunction must burden no more speech than necessary; First Amendment — The qualified public access arrangement survives; First Amendment — The private-property extension lacks the necessary remedial connection; First Amendment — The qualified medical-noise restriction survives; First Amendment — The prohibition on observable images is too broad; First Amendment — Peaceful initial approach cannot depend on advance invitation throughout 300 feet; First Amendment — Residential privacy does not justify this territorial exclusion.

##### The limited scope of geographic and paragraph (4) review

**Holding:** Geographic tailoring of the 36-foot zone is fairly included in the presented challenge to exclusion from peaceful expression in designated zones. Paragraph (4)'s separate noise and images questions receive an exceptional prudential review because their continuing categorical restraint, direct connection to the clinic remedy, existing medical record, adversarial presentation and severable legal correction together justify that limited step; briefing alone does not enlarge the petition.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg: eight. Stevens declines these distinct merits questions.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Izumi Seimitsu Kogyo Kabushiki Kaisha v. U.S. Philips Corp.*, November 30, 1993: applies its strong prudential presumption and exercises its retained exceptional route on the stated combination of circumstances.
- *Yee v. City of Escondido*, question-presented portion: preserves the distinction between a subsidiary argument and a related independent question; usefulness alone does not establish fair inclusion.

##### An injunction binds responsible parties and actual concert, not a viewpoint

**Holding:** An injunction may bind named responsible defendants and persons legally shown to act in concert or participation with them who have notice; notice, presence, sympathy, organizational affinity or a shared viewpoint alone does not establish that relation. This principle does not determine any absent person's concert, contempt or individual defenses, or itself establish the neutral operation of this decree.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part II-A(1), joined by all eight other Justices: nine. The three dissenters on operational neutrality join only this distinct coverage principle.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Regal Knitwear Co. v. NLRB*, 324 U.S. 9: applies actual-concert coverage and the protection of independent strangers; injunction wording does not prejudge individual status.
- *NAACP v. Claiborne Hardware Co.*, 458 U.S. 886: applies protection against responsibility based merely on advocacy, association or another person's unlawful acts.

##### The independent conduct-language claims remain undecided

**Holding:** The Court declines the independent facial and separately framed as-applied challenges to the disputed substantive terms in paragraphs (7), (8) and (9), despite actual adversarial merits briefing, because those additional contextual constructions are neither fairly included in the designated-zone and party-coverage questions nor necessary to their resolution, and no further prudential exception is warranted. This nonreach neither denies the named parties' standing nor approves the provisions' disputed outer applications.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part IV-A, joined by Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg: eight on the Rule 14 ground. Stone-Zsela agrees only in the nonreach disposition for these independently disputed terms and applications and adds no ground.

**Material application:** Paragraph (7) addresses physical abuse, grabbing, intimidation, harassment, touching, pushing, shoving, crowding and assault involving clinic users and persons seeking specified homes, and contains its own invited-contact qualification. Paragraph (8) concerns harassment, intimidation, physical abuse, assault and threats against present or former clinicians, staff, employees or volunteers, without that invited-contact text. Paragraph (9) concerns encouraging, inciting or securing another prohibited act. Their separate terms are not merged. Clinic-property entry and access-obstruction provisions (1) and (2) are unchallenged; no contempt, absent person's liability or separate Cheffer claim is adjudicated.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Izumi*: applies the strong prudential presumption and declines a further exceptional extension; briefing is not petition presentation.
- *R. A. V. v. City of St. Paul*, June 22, 1992, and *Claiborne Hardware*: their protections for expression and facial challenge remain available, without deciding these omitted independent claims.

##### Relief removes invalidated predicates

**Holding:** Paragraph (9) cannot support enforcement for encouraging, inciting or securing conduct prohibited only by a restriction set aside here, and the invalidated exclusion cannot be reinstated simply by relabeling that protected activity under another clause. The private-extension, images, initial-consent and residential-territory predicates must be removed without deciding paragraph (9)'s independent substantive validity.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part IV-B, joined by all eight other Justices: nine at the invalidated-predicate and no-evasion level. This agreement does not settle the permissible content of a new decree.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Claiborne Hardware*: applies the connection among unlawful conduct, responsibility and effective relief; no general incitement holding follows.

##### Remand permits lawful relief without requiring a personal zone

**Holding:** The lower court must conform the decree to this judgment and may consider independently lawful, precisely limited conduct relief supported by the record and the governing constitutional rules. This remand commands neither a replacement post-refusal injunction nor any personal-separation distance; the existing independently lawful restrictions are not automatically dissolved.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part IV-C, joined by Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg: eight. Stone-Zsela dissents from the refusal to require his bounded post-refusal replacement.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Califano v. Yamasaki*: applies remedial limits; it does not supply missing findings or compel a particular replacement injunction.
- *Claiborne Hardware*: preserves the relation between conduct relief, responsibility and protected advocacy.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Florida judgment is affirmed in part, reversed in part and remanded for a conforming decree. The qualified public-access arrangement and medical-noise restriction remain; the private extension, images prohibition, initial-invitation requirement and residential territorial ban must be removed, including dependent use under paragraph (9). The predecessor and independently lawful unreviewed terms are not automatically dissolved. The court may consider precisely fitted lawful conduct relief, but no replacement injunction, personal distance, new sound threshold, damages or contempt judgment is commanded.

### Relief after reversal of governmental-actor status

#### Lebron v. National Railroad Passenger Corp.

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-1525.
**Decided:** February 21, 1995.

For the other questions in this decision, see Federal Courts — Preserved claims and disavowed legal arguments (The Court may consider this included legal argument despite its earlier disavowal); First Amendment — Government-created corporations (Special federal creation and permanent majority appointment make Amtrak governmental).

##### Governmental status requires a remand, not an immediate display order

**Holding:** Reversal of the Second Circuit's governmental-action ground restores the First Amendment claim for adjudication with Amtrak treated as governmental, but does not establish that this rejection was unconstitutional or that Lebron is entitled to mandatory display. The lower courts must address the unreviewed speech and forum questions, lawful relief, and the related disposition of the contract claim and TDI counterclaim under their own governing rules; the district display injunction is not automatically reinstated.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's remand portion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer: eight Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Young v. Northern Illinois Conference of United Methodist Church, decided January 17, 1995: applies the distinction between statutory jurisdiction and legal defeat on the federal merits without extending its ministerial-selection defense.
- Tome v. United States, decided January 10, 1995: uses only its separation of threshold legal error from automatic ultimate relief by analogy; no evidentiary or criminal harmless-error standard is transferred.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court awards no display, damages, final contractual relief, or adjudication of TDI's requested declaration permitting termination. The Second Circuit's dismissal directions must be reconsidered consistently with the restored federal claim and ordinary law governing the separate state-law issues. The lower courts may not treat this actor-status ruling as a merits judgment approving the advertisement or invalidating every political-advertising policy.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Second Circuit is reversed and remanded, 8–1, to adjudicate the unreviewed First Amendment and forum questions with Amtrak treated as governmental and reconsider associated state-law dispositions under their own rules. The display injunction is not automatically reinstated; no final display, damages, contract or TDI termination relief is awarded.

### Scope of public-employee speech relief

#### United States v. National Treasury Employees Union

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-1170.
**Decided:** February 22, 1995.

For the other questions in this decision, see First Amendment — Public-employee honoraria restrictions (A broad prospective honoraria ban requires justification for its actual speech burden).

##### The existing ban is unenforceable throughout the certified class

**Holding:** Relief against the existing §501(b) prohibition extends throughout the certified class of affected Executive Branch employees below GS–16. The Court declines to create a general employment-nexus qualification by moving the series-specific parenthesis in §505(3) into the entire honorarium definition; this class remedy does not establish that every class payment is unrelated or that a law separately and validly restricting connected payments would fail.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part II, joined by Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: five Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Pickering remains the merits framework rather than authority to rewrite statutory coverage. Simon & Schuster and Turner retain their stated financial-burden and evidentiary principles; neither compels a judicially created nexus statute. The remedy preserves the independent force of valid ethics laws and decides no new constitutional rule for congressional or judicial employees.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 505(3)'s series-specific nexus qualification and the travel and charitable-payment conditions are set out with this decision's First Amendment holding. The class remedy does not rewrite those conditions or extend to §501(a)'s distinct outside-income ceiling.

##### The injunction does not adjudicate the claims of unrepresented senior employees

**Holding:** The injunction must be confined to the represented class and the individually represented claim retained here; it may not extend this adjudication to unrepresented Executive employees at GS–16 or above. Removing that nonparty protection does not hold their claims meritless or decide materially different applications of the honoraria ban.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part III-A, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: seven Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Madsen v. Women's Health Center, June 30, 1994, supplies attention to actual persons, claims and the lawful scope of remedial adjustment; it is applied without importing its different clinic facts or an automatic bar on all broader relief in other cases.

##### Crane retains his individual relief without a grade-only remand

**Holding:** Peter G. Crane's existing individual relief is retained; his GS–16 status and resulting exclusion from the certified class do not alone require its removal or a separate remand. This disposition concerns a named respondent under the Government's alternative respondents-plus-class request, not a general constitutional exemption for senior employees or a substantive concession of Crane's entire merits case.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part III-B, joined by Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: five Justices; Stone-Zsela adds a judgment-only sixth vote for Crane's relief.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Madsen's bounded remedial treatment is applied to distinguish the necessary removal of nonparty protection from an unsupported grade-only reopening of an individual judgment. No new universal appellate-waiver rule or general senior-employee First Amendment exemption is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The judgment is affirmed in part, reversed in part and remanded. Nonenforcement of the existing ban throughout the certified below-GS–16 class is preserved by five votes; Crane retains individual relief by six; relief for unrepresented senior employees is removed. Congressional and judicial coverage, §501(a)'s outside-income ceiling and valid independent ethics restrictions are not invalidated. No penalty or compensation award is entered.

### State immunity and bistate compact entities

#### Hess v. Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp., 513 U.S. 30

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-1197.
**Decided:** November 14, 1994.

##### PATH’s actual compact and financial structure does not confer the States’ immunity

**Holding:** A bistate compact entity does not acquire the States’ Eleventh Amendment immunity merely by exercising governmental power: its actual legal, financial and institutional relationship must supply good reason to conclude that the States structured it as their arm and Congress concurred, with state-treasury exposure highly probative but not categorically indispensable. PATH is a distinct, financially self-supporting enterprise responsible for its obligations; these employee suits impose no judgment obligation on either State and do not treat either State itself as a defendant, so PATH lacks that immunity.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stevens), joined by Stone-Zsela, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: six Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Lake Country Estates, Inc. v. Tahoe Regional Planning Agency*, 440 U.S. 391 (1979), applies its entity-specific compact inquiry: exercising state power does not itself make a bistate corporation the States, and the compact’s actual structure matters.
- *Mt. Healthy City School District Board of Education v. Doyle*, 429 U.S. 274 (1977), preserves the distinction between a separate public entity and an arm of a State; a governmental function alone supplies no immunity.
- *Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp. v. Feeney*, 495 U.S. 299 (1990), is distinguished as deciding federal-forum consent while assuming, rather than deciding, PATH’s arm status. Its consent ruling is not an earlier holding that PATH is immune.
- *Hilton v. South Carolina Public Railways Commission*, 502 U.S. 197 (1991), preserves state-court FELA coverage without constructive waiver of federal-forum immunity; no abrogation or waiver rule is enlarged here.
- *Puerto Rico Aqueduct & Sewer Authority v. Metcalf & Eddy, Inc.*, 506 U.S. 139 (1993), preserves immediate review of a qualifying immunity denial without establishing that a particular entity is an immune arm. Its appealability holding does not decide PATH’s status.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not declare every compact entity nonimmune, make a debt disclaimer alone dispositive, decide a materially different entity’s practical dependence, abolish state sovereign immunity or hold that FELA abrogates federal-forum immunity. Negligence, causation, damages, the Boiler Inspection Act merits, every independently applicable nonimmunity limitations defense and hypothetical consent exceptions remain undecided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Both the Hess and Walsh immunity dismissals are reversed and remanded. The Eleventh Amendment bar is removed; ordinary nonimmunity defenses and the separate actions' merits remain undecided.

### Declaratory relief and parallel state proceedings

#### Wilton v. Seven Falls Co.

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-562.
**Decided:** June 12, 1995.

##### Discretion to stay this declaratory action

**Holding:** In a declaration-only state-law coverage action with adequate parallel state proceedings, §2201 and Brillhart permit a reasoned stay without Colorado River's exceptional-circumstances showing. The court must consider the state case's scope, defenses, interested and necessary parties and amenability to process; it retains federal jurisdiction and may resume if the state case fails to resolve the controversy.

**Proposition-level authority:** Justice O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; all eight participants adopt the rule.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Federal substantive declaratory questions, independent coercive claims and cases without parallel proceedings remain open. The filing sequence establishes neither obligatory federal adjudication nor automatic state priority.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Brillhart v. Excess Insurance Co., 316 U.S. 491 (1942): retained and applied to adequate parallel adjudication of a declaration-only state-law dispute.
- Colorado River Water Conservation District v. United States, 424 U.S. 800 (1976): distinguished at the source of adjudicative obligation; its exceptional-circumstances rule remains in its distinct setting.
- Moses H. Cone Memorial Hospital v. Mercury Construction Corp., 460 U.S. 1 (1983): preserves appealability of the effectively deferring stay; finality does not determine the source of remedial discretion.
- Ankenbrandt v. Richards (June 15, 1992): preserves ordinary diversity tort jurisdiction; this discretionary declaration supplies no general abstention license.

##### Appellate review of lawful declaratory discretion

**Holding:** A district court's lawful exercise of §2201 discretion to stay this declaratory action is reviewed for abuse of discretion. That deference does not insulate an error about the legal source of discretion or an unsupported assessment of the actual scope and adequacy of the parallel state proceeding.

**Proposition-level authority:** Justice O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; eight Justices adopt this separate review rule.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Abuse-of-discretion review does not require every comparable action to be stayed or prescribe numerical weights for factors; legal limits and actual adequacy remain reviewable.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Brillhart v. Excess Insurance Co.: applied as a reasoned discretionary appraisal, not an exclusive numerical factor test.
- Colorado River and Moses H. Cone: distinguished from the review of this statutory remedial choice without weakening correction of legal-source errors.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the stay of this declaration-only coverage action, retaining federal jurisdiction and the ability to resume if the adequate state proceeding fails to resolve the controversy. No dismissal with prejudice, coverage declaration or ruling on breach of the advance-notice agreement is entered.

### Judicial review of employment-scope certification

#### Gutierrez de Martinez v. Lamagno

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-167.
**Decided:** June 14, 1995.

##### Review of the scope certification

**Holding:** A Westfall Act scope-of-employment certification is judicially reviewable before substitution of the United States becomes final. Initial substitution on certification does not conclusively establish employment scope; §2679(d)(2) makes certification conclusive for removal only.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy and Breyer; six Justices adopt the statutory review rule.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Diversity independently supplies jurisdiction here. Retention under Article III of a nondiverse removed case after rejection of certification, the section 904/headquarters issues outside the grant, actual scope and tort merits remain undecided. No universal discovery entitlement or specified trial mode is required; the foreign-country exception remains effective.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Abbott Laboratories v. Gardner: applies the judicial-review presumption to an ambiguous statutory finality arrangement.
- United States v. Smith: preserves employee exclusivity after scope is established; does not make the certificate conclusive.
- FDIC v. Meyer: preserves the distinction between immunity, actionable claims and employee exclusivity; supplies no certification-finality rule.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand for judicial review of employment scope under applicable law with appropriate evidence and procedure, and determination of the proper defendant before immunity and merits. No immunity waiver, tort liability or federal damages action is created.

### State-tax equitable relief

#### National Private Truck Council, Inc. v. Oklahoma Tax Commission

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-688.
**Decided:** June 19, 1995.

For the other questions in National Private Truck Council, Inc. v. Oklahoma Tax Commission, decided June 19, 1995, No. 94-688. see Federal Courts — Statutory attorney's fees (Fees follow an available fee-eligible federal claim).

##### Federal tax equity and an actually adequate legal remedy

**Holding:** Section 1983 does not authorize injunctive or declaratory interference with state tax administration where an adequate legal remedy actually protects the claimant. The limitation arises from federal equitable principles informing §1983, not direct application of 28 U.S.C. §1341 to state courts, and does not restrict otherwise lawful state-law equitable relief.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; nine Justices adopt this remedial rule.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Extraordinary relief remains possible for irreparable injury, cloud on title or a genuine risk of numerous suits between the same parties involving the same law or facts. Mere parallel claims by many taxpayers do not suffice. An inadequate remedy, including continued unconstitutional taxation after a refund judgment, may change the equitable inquiry. No general exhaustion rule or withdrawal of a promised refund is authorized; the general state-court duty to entertain section 1983 is assumed, not decided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Dennis v. Higgins: preserves the Commerce Clause right; distinguishes right from equitable relief.
- Dows v. Chicago; Matthews v. Rodgers; Great Lakes Dredge & Dock Co. v. Huffman: apply the tax-equity tradition and its adequacy and extraordinary-injury limits.
- Reich v. Collins: preserves an actually honored postpayment remedy; the successful refunds here do not require duplicate equity.
- Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation: preserves application of governing tax law and meaningful correction, without fixing a single remedy.
- Livadas v. Bradshaw: applies the separation of federal right, enforcement channel and available relief.
- Felder v. Casey; Patsy v. Board of Regents: distinguish hostile state conditions and general exhaustion from this intrinsic federal equitable limitation.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm denial of the reviewed section 1983 injunction/declaration and section 1988 fees while preserving the existing tax invalidity and adequate state-law refund entitlement. No tax sum, separate damages determination, independent state fee ruling or new federal remedial direction follows.

#### Arkansas v. Farm Credit Services of Central Arkansas

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1918.
**Decided:** June 2, 1997.

##### Private production credit associations cannot borrow the sovereign's forum exception

**Holding:** Borrower-owned production credit associations suing in their own right and commercial interest do not acquire the United States' exception to §1341 merely from a federal charter, instrumentality designation or asserted substantive tax immunity. With the conceded plain, speedy and efficient Arkansas remedy, their federal injunction and declaration action must be dismissed for lack of jurisdiction.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Breyer), unanimous on institutional identity, adequacy and injunction; all eight Associates join the declaratory-relief application, with Stone-Zsela concurring in that judgment only.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The entity-specific rule establishes no universal United States-joinder requirement for every agency and decides no Federal Reserve or FDIC suit. The United States' amicus appearance does not make it a plaintiff.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Department of Employment involved the sovereign and Red Cross as co-plaintiffs. Moe involved a separate statutory jurisdictional ground. Nash-Finch involved governmental regulation under §2283. None gives these commercial lenders the sovereign's exception.

##### Conceded state-remedy adequacy requires forum dismissal without tax merits

**Holding:** The associations' express concession that Arkansas supplies a plain, speedy and efficient remedy satisfies §1341's remedial condition on this record. Dismissal determines the federal forum only; the associations' substantive tax privileges and the validity of the taxes remain undecided in this action.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Breyer), unanimous on adequacy and nonreach; eight Associate joins in the separate declaratory application.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Arkansas §§26-18-507 and 26-18-406 remain separate procedures with distinct conditions; no deadline is supplied. Section 2077's instrumentality designation and obligation-specific tax privileges do not confer a sovereign forum, and their substantive application remains undecided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand for dismissal of the federal injunction and declaration action, removing the federal merits judgment. No refund, mandatory joinder, new factfinding or disposition of the parallel state action is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** National Private Truck Council's actual-adequacy limit and Reich's protection against withdrawal of an apparent postpayment remedy survive; neither defect is disputed here.

#### De Buono v. NYSA-ILA Medical and Clinical Services Fund

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1594.
**Decided:** June 2, 1997.

For the provider-tax merits and relief in De Buono v. NYSA-ILA Medical and Clinical Services Fund, June 2, 1997, No. 95-1594, see Labor and Employment — ERISA preemption and hospital-payment measures.

##### The particular ERISA-state-remedy uncertainty defeats the plain-remedy condition

**Holding:** Section 1341 does not bar this prospective ERISA action because the identified affirmative New York remedy is not sufficiently plain at the disputed boundary between that remedy and §1132(e)(1)'s exclusive federal jurisdiction over the fiduciary action. This record-specific determination permits present merits adjudication; it neither exempts ERISA suits generally from §1341 nor authorizes repayment of state taxes.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Part I, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer; six direct joins. Stone-Zsela agrees only in the threshold disposition. Scalia and Thomas would obtain further jurisdictional briefing and argument.

**Material application:** The provider assessment is a tax. Section 1132(a)(3) supplies the fiduciary equitable action; §1132(e)(1) generally makes that action exclusively federal while preserving concurrent jurisdiction for benefit-enforcement and qualified medical-child-support actions. Section 1144(d) preserves other federal law. Uncertainty about the identified affirmative state declaration route at that boundary, rather than assumed jurisdiction, permits prospective merits adjudication.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The holding creates neither a general ERISA exemption from §1341 nor an absolute bar to state consideration of ERISA. State participation cannot confer jurisdiction or waive separate repayment defenses.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Tully's constitutional-tax declaration route and Morgan Guaranty's different administrative refund proceeding do not establish this affirmative HFA remedy. National Private Truck Council and Reich require a real remedy rather than an assumed substitute.

### Qualified-immunity appeals and presentation

#### Doe v. Taylor Independent School District

**Docket or dockets:** Fifth Circuit No. 90-8431; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.
**Decided:** October 16, 1995.

##### Immediate review of legal immunity questions and factual sufficiency

**Holding:** An immediate qualified-immunity appeal may decide a separable legal question on the plaintiff's properly supported assumed facts, but may not decide only whether the evidence is sufficient to establish those facts. A factual-sufficiency portion must be dismissed; an appellate court cannot resolve disputed knowledge or causation by choosing whose account is true and recasting that choice as law.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part I, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No particular additional factual-only appeal is found to exist. The Court resolves neither credibility nor the inconsistent January/February descriptions of Caplinger's first notice.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the separable legal immunity judgment and remand; dismiss any purely factual-sufficiency portion. The distinct supervisory-right and individual-immunity holdings appear under Civil Rights, Doe, October 16, 1995, Fifth Circuit No. 90-8431.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Johnson v. Jones supplies the legal-question/factual-sufficiency boundary. Elder v. Holloway requires examination of all relevant law, not reconstruction of a more favorable factual record for the officer. Neither rule expands collateral appellate jurisdiction.

#### Love v. Pepersack

**Docket or dockets:** Fourth Circuit No. 94-1582; no separately established Supreme Court docket.
**Decided:** December 11, 1995.

##### First-instance determination of an unpleaded personal-immunity defense

**Holding:** The Court declines to decide personal qualified immunity for the first time where the defense was not raised in the dismissal motion or decided below and the exceptional adversarial presentation needed to depart from ordinary practice is not established. Any properly available defense may be asserted in further proceedings subject to ordinary procedural requirements; the Court neither decides its merits nor finds permanent waiver.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part I; all eight Associates join. Stone-Zsela does not join.

**Material application:** Love was not required to anticipate and disprove the affirmative defense in her complaint. Respondents acknowledged not invoking immunity in the dismissal motion and described an answer-stage defense if the complaint survived.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The presentation limitation is discretionary, not jurisdictional. Neither a merits-first nor an immunity-first sequence is required; potential conclusiveness and an unchanged favorable judgment alone do not supply the missing adversarial occasion. Current recognition of a constitutional right supplies no retrospective conduct-date notice by itself.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Gomez and Harlow preserve the official's affirmative-defense pleading responsibility. Singleton and Carlson preserve exceptional discretionary consideration, with Carlson's fully presented, unopposed new question distinguished. Elder concerns relevant law on a preserved immunity issue; Williams a question decided below; X-Citement Video renewed alternatives rejected below; Lebron an included argument for the same preserved claim. Izumi and Zobrest preserve the separate presentation and avoidance limits. Doe and Hodge permit notice-only rulings on actually presented immunity defenses, without compelling this first-instance adjudication.

The due-process fracture, independently unanimous propositions, and arms holdings are under Civil Rights, Love v. Pepersack, December 11, 1995, Fourth Circuit No. 94-1582.

#### Behrens v. Pelletier, 516 U.S. 299

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-1244.

**Decided:** February 21, 1996.

##### A prior appeal does not categorically exhaust legal-immunity review

**Holding:** An earlier pleading-stage qualified-immunity appeal does not, merely because it occurred, bar a later summary-judgment appeal presenting a separable legal immunity question that otherwise satisfies the collateral-order requirements. The later appeal may address legal immunity on properly assumed facts; this rule authorizes neither factual-sufficiency review nor repeated appeals of an unchanged legal ruling or matters resolved by the first mandate.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; seven Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Cohen's cumulative conditions and Mitchell's legal-immunity category apply. Digital Equipment's importance and category discipline remains; Johnson supplies no numerical ceiling and retains its factual boundary.

##### An authorized appeal uses assumed facts and excludes factual sufficiency

**Holding:** Within an otherwise authorized qualified-immunity appeal, the appellate court may decide whether conduct on the District Court's properly assumed or sufficiently supported facts violates clearly established law, but may not choose which factual account is true or reweigh whether evidence supports it. If the relevant assumptions are unstated, the court may identify the likely assumptions favorable to the nonmovant in the manner Johnson permits, without deciding truth or evidentiary sufficiency.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part II, unanimous. Stevens and Breyer condition agreement on an otherwise authorized appeal and do not find successive authorization here.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Johnson and Kimberlin preserve assumed-fact review without factual selection. Elder requires relevant conduct-date law but creates no jurisdiction or retrospective notice. Swint rejects convenience-based expansion; Doe's factual-sufficiency exclusion remains.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No immunity, constitutional violation, property interest, factual truth, evidentiary sufficiency or damages issue is decided.

##### A different surviving claim does not itself defeat a qualifying immunity appeal

**Holding:** The fact that an official must still defend another claim does not, by itself, eliminate an otherwise authorized immediate appeal of a separable legal immunity denial on a different claim. That claim-specific principle neither supplies missing appellate authorization nor makes the other claim reviewable.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part III, joined by all seven other Associate Justices; eight Justices. Stone-Zsela does not join; Stevens and Breyer continue to oppose successive appeal as of right.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Mitchell protects against defending the claim to which immunity applies; Swint prevents a proper appeal from carrying unrelated matters and leaves its necessary-connected-review reservation open.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse only the categorical Ninth Circuit dismissal and remand to identify proper assumptions favorable to the nonmovant and any actually reviewable legal issue, dismissing factual-sufficiency and otherwise unavailable issues. The summary-judgment denial is not reversed, immunity is not awarded, and neither the separate United States negligence claim nor earlier substitution ruling is reopened. No stay or permission is granted.

#### Pinder v. Johnson

**Docket or dockets:** Fourth Circuit No. 93-2125; no Supreme Court docket stated in the Record
**Decided:** May 21, 1996

##### What may an appellate court decide on this separable legal qualified-immunity appeal?

**Holding:** Sections 1254(1) and 1291 permit review of the separable legal immunity issue on the plaintiff-favorable account governing that issue. This jurisdiction does not permit a determination of evidentiary sufficiency, disputed intent or causation, or claims excluded from the appeal.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (O'Connor), Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; unanimous controlling rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** The reviewable question is whether the assumed conduct violated a right sufficiently established when Johnson acted. The district court's characterization of its disposition as one under Rule 12(b)(6), and the appellate court's summary-judgment terminology, do not authorize an unfavorable resolution of disputed facts or enlarge the legal issue before the Court. The Court does not rely on a second-appeal premise or convert a legal immunity appeal into fact review.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court determines neither disputed causation nor ultimate constitutional liability. The substantive notice holding and its exact five-Justice coalition appear under Civil Rights, Affirmative danger and conduct-time qualified immunity.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The reviewed individual-immunity judgment is affirmed, 5-4, on the conduct-time notice ground. No excluded claim or new factual question is decided.

### Scope of federal declaratory and injunctive relief

#### Dalton v. Little Rock Family Planning Services, 516 U.S. 474

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1025.

**Decided:** March 18, 1996.

##### Relief must follow the established federal conflict

**Holding:** When the established Medicaid conflict concerns particular applications of a state funding restriction, federal declaratory and injunctive relief must protect those applications without invalidating or suspending severable applications for which no conflict has been established. The July 25 injunction and July 27 declaration therefore cannot reach Amendment 68 in its entirety. The funding obligation underlying that conclusion is accepted without deciding the first petition question.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion, unanimous on the separate summary merits disposition; no certiorari poll is disclosed.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No new general facial-review rule, constitutional abortion-funding entitlement or authoritative resolution of every Arkansas severability question is announced. The Medicaid funding obligation is left unreviewed, not adopted as a Supreme Court holding. The decision does not adjudicate the separate Nebraska appeal or an independent crime-victim-program challenge.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Harris v. McRae leaves the precise statutory funding issue open. NTEU and Madsen support relief commensurate with the established defect while preserving lawful independent operation; Madsen's speech-specific test is not transferred to funding.

##### Prospective protection follows the applicable obligation

**Holding:** The confined decree must preserve the adjudicated Medicaid protection while Arkansas participates and controlling federal law requires the coverage. It may not suspend unrelated applications merely because the State continues participating, regardless of a later change in the governing conditions. An actual lawful change may support appropriate modification; this decision declares no current lapse or automatic dissolution.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion, unanimous on the separate summary merits disposition; no certiorari poll is disclosed.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Partially vacate and remand to conform both the July 25 whole-amendment injunction and July 27 blanket declaration. Preserve protection against refusal of medically necessary abortions for Medicaid-eligible rape/incest patients while Arkansas participates and applicable federal law requires coverage, including the corresponding protection against inconsistent state-plan provisions. No section may evade that protection. Every severable nonconflicting application remains outside the decree. No damages, past reimbursement, payment for every abortion, continued Medicaid participation, new trial or automatic dissolution is ordered.

**Material application:** Pub. L. 104-91 Title I continues the applicable FY1995 Medicaid authority and conditions. Section 101 retains the current-rate ceiling and applicable lower-rate qualifications when House and Senate amounts differ or only one House has acted. Sections 102–105 preserve the appropriation's extent and manner, prohibit initiation or resumption of projects unavailable in FY1995, exclude newly proposed cross-account conditions absent from the applicable FY1995 Act and cover obligations or expenditures in the authorized period. Unless Title I or the applicable appropriations Act otherwise provides, §106 ends authority at the earliest of an appropriation for the activity, enactment of the applicable appropriations Act without it, or September 30, 1996. Section 110 begins coverage on December 16, 1995; the general continuing-resolution deadline is not this account's deadline. No amount, current lapse or intervening repeal is adjudicated.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Amendment 68 §1 prohibits public abortion funding except to save the mother's life and operates beyond Medicaid; §2's conception-to-birth policy is limited by the Federal Constitution; §3 excludes contraceptives and requires no appropriation. The Court decides neither Title XIX/Hyde's underlying construction nor a direct abortion prohibition. Casey and Fargo's access, proof and remedy standards remain unchanged and create no funding entitlement.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Casey and Fargo retain their actual access holdings; this limited remedial decision neither expands nor revises them.

#### Leavitt v. Jane L.

**Docket or dockets:** 95-1242
**Decided:** June 17, 1996

##### Does invalidity of Utah Code §76-7-302(2) necessarily eliminate subsection (3)?

**Holding:** Section 317 expressly preserves provisions and applications and declares that each would have been enacted despite invalidity elsewhere; it permits §302(3) to operate independently of subsection (2)'s invalid prohibition. The Court of Appeals may not eliminate every subsection (3) application solely from the original comprehensive design or incorporation of subsection (2)'s exceptions. Independent constitutional objections remain separate.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas; five direct joins in this definitive summary merits correction.

**Material application:** Subsection (3) independently prohibits abortion after twenty weeks from conception except when necessary to save the woman's life, prevent grave damage to her medical health, or prevent birth of a child with grave defects, in the attending physician's professional judgment. Those incorporated conditions remain intelligible without subsection (2)'s prohibition. Subsection (3) does not separately incorporate rape or incest exceptions, and its fixed week is not individualized viability.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Not every severability clause is conclusive or surviving application constitutional. Section 317.2 separately reenacts the 1974 §302 if this Court holds §302 as amended by 1991 Senate Bill 23 unconstitutional; it neither withdraws §317 nor is triggered by this state-law correction. No categorical Eleventh Amendment question is accepted or decided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Utah's Salt Lake City v. International Ass'n of Firefighters, 563 P.2d 786; State v. Salt Lake City, 445 P.2d 691; and Carter, 399 P.2d 440, retain their interdependence and inseparable-compromise principles but are distinguished by the explicit independent-enactment direction and separately operable command.

##### What relief follows correction of the appealed inseverability rationale?

**Holding:** Relief must correct that state-law error while preserving necessary protection supported by independently established federal grounds. The courts must determine the lawful declaration and injunction, retain protection against demonstrated unconstitutional previability applications, and address genuinely dependent reporting, certification and sanctions without automatically validating or invalidating them.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam Part II, the same five direct joins; no independent federal violation is newly adjudicated.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Partial summary vacatur and remand, 5–4, only insofar as independently lawful applications are eliminated solely on inseverability grounds. No automatic enforcement or dissolution of independently justified protection follows; relief cannot persist solely on the rejected rationale. Preserved alternative grounds may sustain favorable relief without enlargement, but preservation, proof and federal validity are not decided in the first instance. The unappealed §302(2) invalidation and unreviewed §§307–308 judgment remain untouched; neither becomes a Supreme Court merits holding. No permanent retention of every injunction, new hearing or trial is ordered.

**Limits and questions not reached:** H.B. 206 may be considered only as properly relevant to a live issue, without a direct challenge, assumed repeal or mootness. Its viability method is confined to §310.5, and §314 still identifies §§307–308. Section 315 excuses compliance with §§302, 304(2), 305(2) and 310.5 only for a serious medical emergency leaving insufficient time; it does not include §§307–308. Their distinct survival and grave-health terms receive no assumed saving construction. No fixed week is approved as viability, clinical fact found, or general funding duty created.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Dalton requires correction of excessive relief to preserve independently supported federal protection. Casey's complete protected-choice, six-step interference, clinical and remedial framework remains unchanged; this decision does not adopt a new constitutional framework. Fargo's distinction between notice or clinical clarity and substantive health protection remains. Sioux Falls Clinic v. Miller's relief for its proved indivisible condition does not prove invalidity of every application here.

### State immunity and IGRA officer suits

#### Seminole Tribe of Florida v. Florida, 517 U.S. 44

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-12.
**Decided:** March 27, 1996.

##### IGRA's binding good-faith adjudication is judicial despite later statutory steps

**Holding:** Adjudication of IGRA's federal good-faith negotiation dispute is a judicial function when its judgment conclusively determines legal relations and activates constrained statutory consequences. Later mediation and executive action, and the absence of a guaranteed compact, do not make that judgment advisory.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Part I, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer: seven Justices. Stevens's serious doubt and Stone-Zsela's nonjoin supply no additional support.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Aetna and Nashville permit conclusive adverse declarations without an immediately executable coercive decree. Brimson supports judicial assistance to administration within its adversarial, conclusive, enforceable-order setting, without creating IGRA contempt power. Ferreira's revisable commissioner process is distinguished. Gordon's jurisdictional disposition supplies no delivered reasoned majority opinion requiring execution in every judicial case; Hayburn produced no Supreme Court merits decision, and its reported circuit communications remain historical materials. Wisconsin's partial-redress rule concerns the judicial capacity to afford meaningful relief. Morgan Stanley and Plaut: Article III permits Congress to require reinstatement of qualifying completed private securities limitations dismissals under §27A(b)'s objectively defined procedure while courts independently determine eligibility and adjudicate the claims. The holdings do not establish an unrestricted power to reopen completed merits judgments and do not determine whether IGRA's conclusive adjudication ceases to be judicial because mediation or executive action follows.

##### A connected officer may be sued prospectively under IGRA within its complete statutory limits

**Holding:** Ex parte Young permits prospective official-capacity relief against Governor Chiles for an ongoing violation of IGRA's good-faith negotiation duty, subject to IGRA's full sequence and remedial limits. The State-directed wording and detailed procedure do not exclude that route in this case; they do not authorize greater remedies through the officer.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Parts II–III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer: five Justices. No additional general clear-indication theory commands that coalition.

**Material application:** The Governor's representatives negotiated, supplying connection to the alleged violation without establishing that he alone can bind Florida to every term. Mandatory good faith differs from discretion over compact terms, and nonagreement alone does not prove bad faith.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A qualifying request by the Tribe with jurisdiction over the Indian lands triggers §2710(d)(3)(A)'s good-faith duty; suit under (d)(7)(A)(i) may begin only after 180 days. The effective-compact enforcement action under (A)(ii) and Secretary action under (A)(iii) remain separate. Tribal evidence of no compact and failure to respond or respond in good faith precedes the State's good-faith burden under (B)(ii).

An actual adverse finding triggers an order to State and Tribe to conclude a compact within the first sixty days. The court may consider public interest, safety, criminality, financial integrity and adverse economic effects on existing gaming, and shall treat direct-tax demands on the Tribe or Indian lands as evidence of bad faith. Failure to agree during that period requires last-best proposals to a court-appointed mediator, who selects the proposal best comporting with IGRA, other applicable federal law and the findings and order. Submission to both sides begins a second sixty-day period for State consent; consent produces an agreed compact. Nonconsent triggers notice to the Secretary, who shall prescribe procedures in consultation with the Tribe consistently with the selected proposal, IGRA and relevant state law. The findings/order criterion expressly appears in selection under (iv), not verbatim in the Secretary clause (vii).

State nonparticipation does not automatically select the Tribe's proposal. Failure to agree or participate during the ordered period produces statutory progression, not added contempt or an order compelling agreement; every separate obstruction sanction is not decided. Section (d)(4) creates no new taxing authority but preserves agreed regulatory-cost assessments under (d)(3)(C)(iii), separately from direct-tax demands' evidentiary effect. Absence of other taxing authority does not justify refusing negotiation.

Class III gaming still requires approved tribal legislation, a State permitting such gaming for some person, organization or entity, and an effective compact subject to applicable remedial provisions; licensing and regulation remain distinct. Under (d)(8), disapproval may rest on IGRA, other federal law not relating to jurisdiction over gaming on Indian lands, or federal trust obligations. Forty-five-day inaction means approval only insofar as consistent with IGRA, and Federal Register publication remains necessary. Section 2721 severability supplies neither missing predicates nor an alternative program.
