**Limits and questions not reached:** The statute changes prospective law while preserving judicial factfinding, meaningful federal remedies and completed effects. Supported injury excluded solely by the amended conjunction and independently supported threatened injury remain unresolved.

##### 4. The assumed contract objection creates no further bar

**Holding:** Assuming solely for this objection an independent contract and substantial impairment, rational federal regulation of continuing supervision is permissible where the United States is not a contracting party. Judicial approval and benefit to a State do not make the United States a party.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part IV has the support of Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Stone-Zsela agrees only in rejecting the objection.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The premise establishes no actual agreement or cancellation of accrued liability.

##### 5. Remand must supply the missing statutory adjudication

**Holding:** A court that has rejected termination solely on constitutional grounds must determine the statutory predicate and preservation conditions before ending or retaining the disputed relief. Ordinary lawful procedure must permit the material proof needed for that inquiry, without a hearing requirement in every case or a presumed outcome.

**Limits and questions not reached:** This inquiry was never performed, unlike Benjamin’s actual refusal of current evidence. Library relief requires actual access injury and remedial fit; the supplement establishes neither injury nor its absence. The unrenewed Rules Enabling Act issue, automatic stay, new-relief rules, state-court application, prisoner release and independent contract enforcement are not decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirmed and remanded unanimously for actual statutory adjudication. Neither immediate termination nor unconditional continuation is directed. The court must permit necessary material proof without a universal hearing entitlement or a presumed current violation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Rouse; Benjamin:** Applied to amended conditions, constitutional objections, prospective duties, completed effects, and the assumed-contract ground; Benjamin's proof-refusal remedy is distinguished.
- **Plyler:** Preserves the separate statutory route and its original-text limits.
- **Rufo:** Retains its significant-change and tailored-modification requirements for the independent equitable route.
- **Lewis v. Casey:** Requires actual access injury and fit for library remedies; its original statutory scope remains intact.
- **Kokkonen:** Preserves the judicial-command/private-agreement distinction and independent enforcement requirements.
- **Morgan Stanley; Plaut:** Preserve bounded reinstatement and independent adjudication, without resolving all reopening questions.
- **Robertson; Wheeling Bridge; Klein:** Preserve genuine changed-law adjudication and forbid legislative substitution for the judicial task.
- **Beach; Heller; Romer:** Supply rational review tied to the actual classification and legitimate ends.
- **National Railroad Passenger Corp. v. Atchison:** Supplies the limited federal-nonparty contract framework.

### Factual immunity appeals and independent appellate sanctions authority

#### Berryman v. Rieger

**Docket or dockets:** Sixth Circuit No. 96-2337; no separate Supreme Court docket established.
**Decided:** 1998-08-03.

##### The actual appeal sought factual selection rather than a separable legal immunity decision

**Holding:** The Sixth Circuit properly dismissed this interlocutory appeal because the arguments actually pursued required choosing disputed defense evidence over the plaintiff's sworn account rather than deciding a separable legal immunity question on the proper assumptions. An otherwise authorized legal issue accepting those assumptions remains reviewable; neither the presence of factual disputes nor a prior appeal categorically defeats jurisdiction, and this first mandate expressly left proper immunity presentation open.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of O'Connor's Opinion of the Court, joined by all eight other Justices; nine direct joins to the complete rule and application.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The dismissal rests on the arguments actually pursued, not assumptions about an unrecovered letter. The preliminary Clerk's order did not finally determine jurisdiction; the earlier mandate expressly reserved proper immunity presentation. No one-appeal limit is created, and a permitted motion cannot authorize factual-sufficiency review.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Johnson, Doe, Behrens, Smith and Camilo apply the legal/factual review boundary without a categorical disputed-facts rule or an invented earlier immunity adjudication.

##### Section 1912 did not authorize the award after the circuit dismissed the appeal

**Holding:** Section 1912 permits a discretionary award to the prevailing party of just damages for delay and single or double costs when the Supreme Court or a court of appeals affirms a judgment. Because the Sixth Circuit dismissed this appeal rather than affirming a judgment, that provision did not authorize its double-cost and attorney-fee award; the award and its derivative amount-setting entitlement are vacated.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of O'Connor's opinion; all nine Justices join the statutory rule and this disposition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** This Court's affirmance of the dismissal does not retroactively satisfy the circuit's missing statutory trigger. The full measure of §1912 damages when the provision applies is not decided, and no replacement award is imposed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Section 1912 is applied according to its affirmed-judgment condition; Cooter & Gell's source-specific treatment of appellate sanctions does not permit discretion to cure an unavailable source of authority.

##### Sanctions may be reconsidered only under independently valid authority and process

**Holding:** On remand the Sixth Circuit may, without being required to do so, reconsider sanctions under independently valid authority and its necessary conditions. Rule 38 permits an award to the appellee of just damages, including appropriate appellate attorney fees, and single or double costs only upon a determination that the appeal is frivolous, after a separately filed motion or notice from the court and a reasonable opportunity to respond; an amount-setting opportunity after an award is imposed does not by itself supply an earlier opportunity to contest entitlement.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of O'Connor's opinion, joined in full by all eight other Justices; nine direct joins to the limited remand and every stated condition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A request in a brief is not a separate motion, and jurisdictional show-cause process is not automatically sanctions notice; the Court does not find other adequate process absent. Dismissal alone proves no frivolousness. Inherent fee power still requires bad faith and fair process; §1927 concerns counsel, while Rule 39 ordinary costs remain distinct. No alternative authority is deemed exercised, sanction imposed, amount fixed or hearing required.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Rule 38's operative conditions and Roadway Express's process requirements govern possible reconsideration; Cooter & Gell preserves source-specific authority, and Chambers retains its existing limits without automatic substitution or enlargement.

##### Jurisdictional dismissal and sanctions reconsideration decide no underlying constitutional claim

**Holding:** The action returns for ordinary proceedings on the distinct Eighth Amendment medical-care and First Amendment retaliation claims, with each defendant's personal conduct and independent defenses unresolved. Neither the appeal's dismissal nor the sanctions ruling establishes a constitutional violation, serious medical need, deliberate indifference, protected litigation, retaliatory causation, ultimate immunity, liability, damages or a right to trial, discovery or a special hearing.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part IV of O'Connor's opinion; all nine Justices join these nonreach and remedial limits. No independent constitutional merits holding is adopted.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Defendants' disputed accounts and alleged forgery remain unproved. Crawford-El and Mt. Healthy preserve ordinary competent proof and the separate preponderance same-decision defense; a possible lawful explanation is insufficient. The September 1992 complaint is an outer bound, not a precise treatment date, and later decisions create no earlier notice.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Crawford-El and Mt. Healthy retain their ordinary proof and separate causal burdens; Hunter, Elder and the existing immunity cases retain their procedural and conduct-time limits without a new merits application.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The jurisdictional dismissal remains effective. The double-cost and attorney-fee award and its derivative direction to compute the enhanced entitlement are vacated. The Sixth Circuit may examine its record and exercise lawful sanctions discretion under independently proper authority, providing any required process before a new award; no new award or particular hearing is commanded. The underlying action returns for ordinary proceedings without an automatic trial, discovery plan, constitutional ruling or damages award. Ordinary dismissal costs remain separate, and no amount or new Supreme Court costs award is decided.

### Identifying proper assumptions in legal immunity appeals

#### Camilo-Robles v. Hoyos

**Docket or dockets:** First Circuit Nos. 97-2260, 97-2261, 97-2262 and 97-2264; no separate Supreme Court docket established.
**Decided:** 1998-07-20.

For the other questions decided in this same case, docket and date, see Civil Rights (questions: Puerto Rico statutory protection and federal contractor immunity are distinct questions; Each appellant's own culpable causal conduct and conduct-time notice govern; Hoyos's legal immunity request fails on his own assumed information and effective clearance; Rivera-Gonzalez's actual reading and professional decision require separate treatment; Diaz-Pagan's warning and absence do not erase a supported earlier causal omission; Santiago-Gonzalez's own assignment authority supplies the alleged causal role; Toledo-Davila's reliance on the clearance is relevant but not conclusive; The denials preserve ordinary proceedings and each act's temporal limits).

##### The separable legal immunity questions are reviewable on the proper assumed account

**Holding:** A lawful interlocutory immunity appeal may resolve a separable legal question on the District Court's proper plaintiff-favorable assumptions, but cannot choose factual truth or reweigh evidentiary sufficiency. When a brief order leaves those assumptions unstated, the appellate court may identify the likely favorable account from material already before the District Court; it must exclude extra-record material and dismiss any genuinely factual-sufficiency portion. The court may seek clarification if a necessary assumption cannot responsibly be identified.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Scalia). Part I of Scalia's Opinion of the Court; all eight other Justices join this legal-review boundary and its application here.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The unfinished Rule 56(f) process and missing completed oppositions supply no default, admission or waiver. The available trial-record materials support the bounded assumed account; no further factual-only portion is identified. The state-defense ruling creates no universal immediate appeal from every claimed absolute immunity.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Johnson, Doe, Behrens and Smith retain their assumed-facts/factual-sufficiency boundary; no appellate jurisdiction is enlarged.

### Legal review of separate employment-immunity grounds

#### Brewster v. Board of Education

**Docket or dockets:** Ninth Circuit No. 97-55203; no separate Supreme Court docket established.
**Decided:** 1998-08-17.

For the other questions decided in this same case, docket and date, see Civil Rights (questions: Contextual balancing neither grants nor defeats immunity automatically; The actual salary process supports personal immunity for reduction and reported recovery; Personal proof, claim boundaries and ordinary proceedings remain distinct); First Amendment (questions: The assumed attendance-speech account defeats the common legal immunity ground).

##### The separable legal immunity grounds are reviewable on the proper assumptions

**Holding:** The Court may review the circuit's legal immunity judgment under §1254(1) on the facts properly assumed for the interlocutory appeal; it may not choose between disputed accounts or decide evidentiary sufficiency. Disputed facts do not categorically foreclose an otherwise authorized legal appeal, and an unadjudicated personal-evidence fallback cannot be resolved by relabeling it a legal defense.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Breyer). Part I of Breyer's Opinion of the Court; all nine Justices join this review boundary and its present application.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Furuno motive concession, absence of a same-decision assertion, assumed salary interest and reported withholding govern this legal review without resolving trial truth. Other personal-evidence fallbacks remain undecided; no hypothetical factual appeal is invented. Section 1254 does not expand §1291 authority.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Mitchell preserves legal immunity review.
- Johnson excludes factual-sufficiency review.
- Doe applies that exclusion.
- Behrens preserves authorized assumed-fact review.
- Smith applies it to the concrete account.
- Camilo preserves defendant-specific assumptions.
- Berryman is distinguished by the actual factual arguments there.

### Prison decree termination and actual amended findings

#### Hadix v. Johnson

**Docket or dockets:** Sixth Circuit Nos. 96-2463 and 96-2582.

**Decided:** 1998-09-14.

##### The amended statutory findings must actually be adjudicated

**Holding:** The movant must establish that covered prospective relief was entered without substantive findings of necessity, narrowness and least intrusiveness under §3626(b)(2); sufficient findings need no ritual statutory wording. If that termination route applies, amended §3626(b)(3) preserves relief only on written, record-based findings that it remains necessary to correct a current **and** ongoing federal-right violation, extends no further than necessary, is narrowly drawn, and is the least intrusive necessary means.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Scalia), Parts I–III, unanimous.

**Material application:** Rouse, Benjamin and Gavin supply the amended conditions. The November 26, 1997 amendment applies to pending proceedings; the former current-or-ongoing text addressed by Lewis v. Casey and Plyler is not the present standard. Rufo v. Inmates of Suffolk County Jail retains its distinct significant-change and suitably tailored modification rule under Rule 60(b)(5), but that threshold is not an extra prerequisite to the enacted termination route. Consent decrees are covered relief. Negotiated protections do not become independent federal substantive rights merely because they appear in a decree. Equally, a necessary corrective remedy may exceed the minimum substantive conduct rule when all required findings support it. Neither the lack of original findings nor a term's breadth alone proves current compliance or requires unconditional termination. Each retained obligation must satisfy the preservation conditions; unsupported future obligations must end. A case in which the amended conjunction alone excludes an actually supported violation protected by the former safeguard remains undecided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Rouse's amended-text application controls, as applied in Benjamin and Gavin. Lewis and Plyler retain the actual original-text holdings they made. Rufo remains a distinct equitable route, neither displaced nor added to the statutory prerequisites.

##### The existing remand supplies the unperformed inquiry

**Holding:** Because the District Court denied termination solely on constitutional grounds, the Sixth Circuit's reversal and return for the statutory inquiry are affirmed, with further proceedings consistent with the amended conditions. The court must permit the material proof needed to decide those conditions; this judgment orders neither immediate wholesale termination nor indefinite unconditional continuation of the decree.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Scalia), Parts I–III, unanimous.

**Material application:** Gavin requires the adjudication a constitutional-only denial left undone. Benjamin involved an actual legal refusal to receive relevant current evidence; no identical refusal is established here. Rouse involved substantive evaluation of findings and monitoring reports; those evaluated findings cannot be imported into this case. Ordinary lawful procedure must allow the evidence necessary for the real inquiry, but an unreproduced decree or the possibility of useful additional information creates no freestanding hearing entitlement. The Court decides no current Michigan condition or individual obligation. The automatic-stay issue belongs to separate appeal No. 96-1943; no new stay, expiration or statutory clock follows here. Due process and equal protection, mentioned in the lower court's footnote as grounds not before it, are not enlarged into holdings. Independent contracts, state enforcement, prisoner release, new-relief standards and complete dismissal of the original action are likewise outside this decision.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Gavin's missing-inquiry rule applies. Benjamin's necessary-proof rule is preserved without importing its factual predicate; Rouse's rejection of a speculative compulsory inquiry remains intact.

Related questions in Hadix v. Johnson, 1998-09-14, dockets Sixth Circuit Nos. 96-2463 and 96-2582, appear under Constitutional Structure — Congressional amendment of prospective prison relief.

### Removal jurisdiction and claim-specific state immunity

#### Wisconsin Department of Corrections v. Schacht, 524 U.S. 381

**Docket or dockets:** No. 97-461.

**Decided:** June 22, 1998.

##### Immunity affecting one claim does not eliminate removal jurisdiction over the remaining federal claims

**Holding:** An action presenting substantial federal claims within §1331 is removable under §1441(a) despite an Eleventh Amendment bar applicable to particular State or official-capacity claims. The bar prevents adjudication of the affected claim when properly asserted, but does not erase otherwise valid original and removal jurisdiction over the remaining federal claims or require remand of the entire action under §1447(c).

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Material application:** The actual complaint supplies substantial federal claims independently of immunity. A bar affecting State and official-capacity claims does not eliminate the federal basis for the personal-capacity claims, unlike a failure of complete diversity when diversity is the sole jurisdictional basis.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Even assuming immunity counts as subject-matter jurisdiction for this analysis, §1447(c)’s direction to remand a case lacking such jurisdiction does not require erasing the independent federal claims. The statute’s separate thirty-day rule for a nonjurisdictional removal-defect motion creates no new defect or timeliness finding in this litigation. The Court need not decide the general jurisdictional classification of Eleventh Amendment immunity.

Removal authority must be established and immunity and any genuinely remaining remand questions treated at their proper claim-specific scope. The actual complaint contains federal claims; City of Chicago’s supplemental-claim discussion is an analogy, not an additional claim here. Wisconsin’s waiver by removing, the merits of the personal-capacity claims, any new relief against the State and compulsory retention of every hypothetical supplemental claim remain undecided. The unappealed dismissals stay outside the returned appeal.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Seventh Circuit’s whole-action remand is vacated. The personal-capacity appeal returns for consideration under the correct jurisdictional framework and otherwise applicable law. No new merits relief, reinstatement, damages or claim against Wisconsin is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Hafer v. Melo:** Its personal/official-capacity distinction is applied; state immunity does not independently bar a personally payable judgment, and personal defenses and merits remain separate.
- **City of Chicago v. International College of Surgeons:** Its substantial-federal-claim removal anchor is applied. Its supplemental-jurisdiction and retention rules create no additional actual claim here.
- **Seminole Tribe of Florida v. Florida:** Its actual statutory and remedial holdings and unresolved direct-State rationale remain unchanged; this decision adopts no general abrogation theory.
- **Pennsylvania v. Union Gas Co.:** Its existing force is undisturbed; the Court neither overrules it nor decides direct-State amenability in this case.
- **Gutierrez de Martinez v. Lamagno:** Its distinct certification, substitution and removal scheme is not transplanted into ordinary federal-question removal.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Lapides v. Board of Regents of University System of Georgia, May 13, 2002, resolves the reserved removal-waiver question for voluntarily removed state-law claims for which the State permitted suit in its own courts. Schacht’s claim-specific jurisdictional holding remains unchanged.

### Review concerns Maine’s separate state-court judgment

#### Alden v. Maine

**Docket or dockets:** No. 98-436; 527 U.S. 706.
**Decided:** 1999-06-23.

##### Review concerns Maine’s separate state-court judgment

**Holding:** Under 28 U.S.C. §1257, the Court may review the federal immunity question decided by Maine’s highest court in this independently filed state action. This review neither reopens nor adjudicates the separate federal proceeding in Mills.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; all nine Justices.

**Precedent treatment:** South Central Bell is applied to the §1257 review channel. Its distinction between appellate review and a new federal damages suit remains unchanged.

##### Maine’s immunity cannot defeat the expressly enacted state-court remedy

**Holding:** Congress, acting within its valid commerce authority, may displace Maine’s asserted nontextual immunity from this expressly authorized private FLSA action in a competent state court. Article VI requires the court to adjudicate that valid federal remedy despite Maine’s lack of consent.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer; five Justices adopt this constitutional ground as well as reversal.

**Precedent treatment:** Garcia, Testa and Howlett are applied for their distinct regulatory and judicial-enforcement propositions. Hilton’s enforcement principle is extended beyond its retained FELA setting to this express FLSA remedy; its own holding and reasons remain intact. Hans, New York and Printz are distinguished within their actual decided reach. Seminole remains without a controlling general State-amenability rule, and Union Gas retains its actual judgment and five-vote constitutional conclusion without converting its plurality rationale into a general test.

##### The immunity dismissal is reversed without adjudicating wages

**Holding:** Maine’s immunity judgment is reversed, and the case is remanded for ordinary adjudication of the federal overtime claims, including coverage, compensation, defenses and legally available relief. The reversal establishes no wage violation or present entitlement to an award.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Part III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer; five Justices. O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas would affirm on immunity.

**Precedent treatment:** Hilton’s distinction between opening the state forum and deciding compensation is followed. The existing limits on retrospective officer relief are unchanged; no substitute officer remedy is created.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Garcia v. San Antonio Metropolitan Transit Authority:** Applied to the validity of generally applicable federal wage regulation of state employers; it is not treated as independently deciding every remedy.
- **Testa v. Katt:** Applied to the obligation of competent state courts to enforce valid federal law.
- **Howlett v. Rose:** Applied after independently resolving the State’s immunity objection. Its governmental defendant was not the State itself, and its holding is not recast as a prior decision of this constitutional issue.
- **Hilton v. South Carolina Public Railways Commission:** Its state-court enforcement principle is extended to the express FLSA remedy. Its retained FELA coverage construction, unchanged text and specialized compensation reliance remain intact; no waiver finding replaces those grounds.
- **Seminole Tribe of Florida v. Florida:** Its statutory-authorization distinction is applied; the decision supplied no controlling direct-State constitutional amenability rule or general Article I prohibition. Its connected prospective officer remedy remains confined to its own statute and conditions.
- **Pennsylvania v. Union Gas Co.:** Its actual damages judgment, statutory construction and five-vote constitutional conclusion remain in force within their decided scope. No broader plurality rationale becomes binding here.
- **Hans v. Louisiana:** Its federal-court judgment is distinguished; it does not establish the asserted immunity against this state-court FLSA remedy. The Court does not overrule its actual result or decide federal-court amenability.
- **New York v. United States:** Its prohibition on compulsory state regulation or ownership of private obligations remains intact; generally applicable regulation and the distinct obligations of state courts remain separate.
- **Printz v. United States; Mack v. United States:** The confined permission for temporary executive records assistance and its unresolved fiscal questions remain unchanged. Neither determines this state-court treasury action.
- **South Central Bell Telephone Co. v. Alabama:** Applied to the authority to review a federal question in a State’s own judgment under §1257.
- **Fitzpatrick v. Bitzer and Ex parte Young:** Their distinct §5 and prospective connected-officer channels are not altered or newly established for these employees. No alternative suit or entitlement is found.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A competent state court must hear the expressly authorized FLSA action against Maine notwithstanding its lack of consent. The new constitutional holding rejects an unalterable state-forum immunity defense to this validly enacted remedy; it does not rest on constructive waiver or on a previously settled general Article I rule. The FLSA’s substantive and remedial conditions still determine whether these employees recover anything.

The prior distinction between statutory authorization and constitutional power remains. This decision resolves that constitutional question for the state-court FLSA action while leaving general federal-court amenability, other statutes and other remedial arrangements undecided. The independent Mills proceeding is unchanged. No new officer or sovereign enforcement action, fiscal defense, burden shift, damages presumption or special procedure is created.

**Operative remedy or transition:** **Judgment:** Reversed and remanded, 5–4. **Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. **Opposing:** O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas, who would affirm.

The competent state court must adjudicate the federal overtime claims under ordinary FLSA requirements, including coverage, compensation, defenses and relief. No overtime, liquidated damages, fees or other award is ordered. The Court does not reinstate the State’s limitations argument rejected below, adjudicate all remaining defenses, reopen Mills, direct an officer or sovereign action, require a new hearing or set a special mandate date.


For the other questions in Alden v. Maine, decided 1999-06-23, at the dockets stated above, see Labor and Employment.

### Congress expressly authorized federal suit and otherwise available remedies

#### College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board

**Docket or dockets:** No. 98-149; October Term 1998.
**Decided:** 1999-06-23.

##### Congress expressly authorized federal suit and otherwise available remedies

**Holding:** Sections 1122(a)–(b) and 1125(a)(1)(B), (a)(2) unmistakably subject States and their instrumentalities to federal suit and otherwise available legal and equitable remedies for covered Lanham Act violations occurring on or after October 27, 1992. That enacted intent establishes neither constitutional power, State consent, a statutory violation nor entitlement to a particular award.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Stone-Zsela's opinion of the Court, joined by all nine Justices.

##### This own-product-advertising theory does not establish the required §5 basis

**Holding:** The alleged loss of customers from Florida Prepaid's misdescription of its own product does not establish a constitutional entitlement to prospective customers or truthful competitive self-advertising, and the statutory tort and economic injury alone do not identify a constitutional deprivation to which this §5 remedy is fitted. Section 5 therefore does not sustain the asserted application on this theory; established business assets, goodwill and legal claims are not categorically excluded from constitutional property.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of Stone-Zsela's opinion of the Court, directly joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Stevens disagrees. The eight-Justice proposition is controlling despite the different judgment votes.

##### Continued ordinary commerce is not unequivocal consent to federal suit

**Holding:** A State's continued operation of an otherwise lawful commercial undertaking does not unequivocally submit it to federal jurisdiction merely because Congress clearly and prospectively subjects that activity to private federal enforcement, even when the undertaking is realistically optional. Congress's legislative intent is distinct from the State's voluntary decision to consent.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of Stone-Zsela's opinion of the Court, directly joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas. Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer disagree.

##### The reversal has no common affirmative amenability rationale

**Authority of the judgment:** Five Justices reverse the federal immunity dismissal. Stone-Zsela relies on Article I and an included-argument view of review scope; Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer rely on prospective commercial consent, and Stevens adds §5. These are distinct affirmative grounds, not five joins in any one of them.

**Limits of the judgment:** The Part V judgment binds the parties and permits the federal claim to return for ordinary adjudication. Parts I–III retain their direct majority support. No Marks rule makes the four-Justice consent position controlling: a participating majority expressly rejects that operative consent proposition, and consent and unilateral congressional displacement depend on different legal predicates. Stevens's §5 ground likewise cannot displace the eight-Justice rejection. No common constitutional or review-scope rule is extracted by combining unused alternatives.

The decision neither sustains nor rejects a general Article I abrogation power. It reaches no litigation-waiver question, particular accepted-benefit condition, new officer claim, underlying advertising merits or separate patent issue. A prospective officer action would require a connected officer, ongoing federal violation, proper claim and genuinely prospective relief; Edelman's retrospective-compensation boundary and Coeur d'Alene's actual limits remain.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Union Gas:** Its private damages judgment, statutory holding and five-vote constitutional conclusion remain effective within their decided scope. No majority here extends or overrules that conclusion, and Brennan's entire plurality does not become a majority rule.
- **Seminole Tribe:** Its clear-authorization distinction is applied. It established no controlling direct-State amenability rule or categorical Article I prohibition and did not overrule Union Gas.
- **Parden, Welch and Hilton:** The residual inference of federal-forum consent from ordinary commercial participation is rejected even under the new explicit enactment. Parden's independently retained substantive FELA coverage and Hilton's specialized state-court enforcement and reliance holding remain intact; no wholesale overruling of those retained propositions occurs.
- **Beers, Great Northern and Atascadero:** Their requirement of State consent supplies the distinction between unmistakable congressional intent and unequivocal State submission.
- **Boerne, South Carolina v. Katzenbach, Katzenbach v. Morgan and City of Rome:** The enforcement/redefinition boundary is applied while retaining real preventive legislation reaching nonviolations and each precedent's constitutional setting. No new numerical or formal legislative-record requirement is imposed.
- **Roth, Paul, Haddle and Sullivan:** Their distinctions between constitutional entitlement, statutory injury and fair adjudication are applied without denying established intangible property or converting every statutory tort into a constitutional deprivation. Logan's protection of a legal claim does not equate the underlying alleged commercial injury with a completed due-process violation.
- **Hans and Fitzpatrick:** Their existing force is unchanged. The separate federal-question/common-law account does not become a Court holding, and the distinct Reconstruction power remains available on its proper predicates.
- **Izumi, Caspari, Madsen, Lebron and X-Citement Video:** Their distinct question-presentation rules remain. There is no majority determination that this Article I argument is fairly included or exceptionally reviewable. Murphy, Kolstad and Olmstead retain their actual scope applications rather than creating an automatic rule for every alternative source of congressional power.
- **Hess, Lane, Blue Fox, Schacht, Cedar Rapids and West:** Their arm-status, remedy-specific consent, removal and accepted-funding distinctions remain confined to their actual settings. They establish neither Florida's commercial consent nor this claim's liability.
- **Ex parte Young, Edelman and Coeur d'Alene:** Their defendant, ongoing-violation and prospective-relief requirements remain; this judgment creates no officer defendant or substitute damages action.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank, June 23, 1999, Part III, resolves the Article I question left open here: Congress may authorize private enforcement against States when the underlying obligation and enforcement measure fall within its Article I authority and Congress unmistakably authorizes the State's exposure to suit, and Article I independently sustains that patent action. Hans supplies no immunity Congress cannot displace in that setting; see Federal Courts — Congress unmistakably authorized this federal patent action against States.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Effective June 23, 1999, the federal immunity dismissal is removed in this action, but no common affirmative rationale supplies a general rule permitting other direct-State Lanham suits. The express statutory authorization remains distinct from constitutional power, State consent and entitlement to relief.

The Court adds a controlling rejection of the proposed explicit prospective commercial-participation consent theory and a confined rejection of the asserted own-product-advertising §5 basis. It preserves actual voluntary consent, fitted preventive §5 legislation and Hilton's retained FELA state-court coverage. Union Gas remains precedent at its decided scope; neither a categorical Article I permission nor prohibition emerges here. Lower courts must respect the direct majority propositions and the mandate without treating the four-Justice consent theory, the one-Justice Article I theory or Stevens's separate §5 theory as a unified holding.

**Operative remedy or transition:** **Reversed and remanded, 5–4.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.

**Opposing:** O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas.

The reversal removes only the federal Lanham immunity dismissal. Falsity, materiality, the statutory cause, commercial injury, causation, preserved defenses and legally available relief remain for ordinary adjudication. No damages, injunction, finding of deceit or special evidence proceeding is ordered. The judgment revives neither the abandoned common-law count nor the dismissed, unappealed counterclaim and decides no part of the separate patent action.

### The requested lien supplies substitute compensation without an applicable waiver

#### Department of the Army v. Blue Fox, Inc.

**Docket or dockets:** No. 97-1642
**Decided:** 1999-01-20.

##### The requested lien supplies substitute compensation without an applicable waiver

**Holding:** Section 702 does not waive federal immunity for an equitable lien whose function is substitute payment from Government property for a private debtor's default when no identified retained property belonging to the claimant or specific statutory payment entitlement supports the requested recovery. The equitable form and identification of a project account do not establish consent; specific enforcement of an actual entitlement remains a different question under its own governing law.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Opinion of the Court has all nine Justices' direct joins and controls the rule, application and limits.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Bowen v. Massachusetts, 487 U.S. 879:** Preserved and distinguished. Its specific statutory payment entitlement differs from compensation for this private debt; money alone is not the classification rule.
- **United States v. Nordic Village, Inc.:** Applied to require consent for the particular affirmative recovery and to reject equitable or property labels as substitutes. Genuine identified-property proceedings retain their own facts and authority.
- **Lane v. Peña:** Applied to separate a substantive governmental duty from waiver of immunity for the actual monetary remedy.
- **Henningsen v. United States Fidelity & Guaranty Co., 208 U.S. 404:** Distinguished as a surety/subrogation setting; it supplies no direct Army waiver for this subcontractor.
- **Pearlman v. Reliance Insurance Co., 371 U.S. 132:** Distinguished for its surety/property relationship; it does not establish Blue Fox's entitlement or consent here.
- **United States v. Munsey Trust Co., 332 U.S. 234:** Preserved in its governmental/surety setting; it creates no general right for an unpaid subcontractor to attach Army funds free of immunity.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Courts must identify the claimant's actual entitlement and the operation of the requested remedy before treating a monetary demand as specific relief under §702. A private-debt substitute does not become authorized merely through an equitable lien, a named project account, notice or the Government's failure to obtain a required bond. A genuine identified-property claim, enacted payment mandate, properly founded Tucker Act claim, recovery against an actual Miller Act surety or another unequivocal waiver remains governed by its own requirements. None is awarded here.

**Operative remedy or transition:** **Reversed and remanded, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The Army lien claim must be dismissed. The Court orders no payment, recreated project balance, replacement lien or tracing hearing. The SBA judgment is untouched. A properly founded alternative remedy remains subject to its own governing law; no existing surety recovery or direct Tucker Act entitlement is established here.

### Congress unmistakably authorized this federal patent action against States

#### Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank

**Docket or dockets:** No. 98-531.
**Decided:** 1999-06-23.

##### Congress unmistakably authorized this federal patent action against States

**Holding:** Sections 271(h) and 296 expressly subject States and their instrumentalities to private patent actions in federal court for covered violations on or after October 28, 1992. Section 296(b) makes the otherwise available patent remedies available on their own statutory terms; that authorization does not itself establish constitutional power, State consent, infringement, or entitlement to a particular award.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part I, joined by all nine Justices, directly adopts this statutory proposition and its limits.

##### Appropriate respondent alternatives are legally available within their limits

**Holding:** A respondent may defend an unchanged favorable judgment on an appropriately supported alternative that neither enlarges its rights nor reduces the adversary's rights under that judgment. CSB's posture permits consideration of its preserved defenses of the immunity denial, but preservation and briefing alone do not expand the §5 question or establish an alternative's merits.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I has all nine Justices' joins at this precise level. Their actual decisions on the alternative grounds remain distinct.

##### Section 5 independently sustains the authorized patent action

**Holding:** Section 5 permits Congress to prevent unconstitutional property deprivations through congruent and proportional enforcement legislation reaching some conduct that is not independently unconstitutional. Sections 271(h) and 296 validly authorize this patent action on that basis; the plaintiff need not establish a separate constitutional violation to proceed under the Act.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, states an independently sufficient holding of the Court. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas disagree.

##### Article I independently sustains the expressly authorized action

**Holding:** Congress may authorize private enforcement against States when the underlying obligation and enforcement measure fall within its Article I authority and Congress unmistakably authorizes the State's exposure to suit. The Patent Clause, together with the Necessary and Proper Clause, supports this federal patent remedy as an independent ground for sustaining the immunity denial.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, states an independently sufficient holding of the Court. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas oppose the extension.

##### Continued commercial participation does not supply consent to this suit

**Holding:** A State's continued commercial participation after Congress authorizes suit does not constitute consent: Florida Prepaid did not consent to federal patent suit by continuing its prepaid-tuition program after §§271(h) and 296 took effect. Actual consent through a distinct State choice, such as an express waiver or voluntary invocation of federal jurisdiction, remains available. Whether Florida Prepaid's litigation conduct waived immunity need not be decided, and no waiver finding is made.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part IV, joined by all nine Justices, applies College Savings Part III and states these limits. Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, who did not join College Savings Part III, accept it as controlling. Neither constitutional holding depends on consent.

##### Limits and questions not reached

The Court makes no finding of patent validity, infringement, willfulness, Florida-remedy adequacy, constitutional injury, or entitlement to relief. Section 296(b) retains its own conditions and any properly presented award-specific constitutional limits. Neither constitutional holding nor the judgment preapproves every compensation, injunction, fee, or enhanced award. Kolstad's Title VII punishment rules do not determine patent enhancement.

The appearance, transfer request under §1404(a), and request for affirmative declarations of invalidity, noninfringement, and unenforceability remain distinct litigation-consent contentions. The Court finds no waiver from any of them. Schacht does not settle their effect. No actual-violation exception, willfulness exception, State-remedy exhaustion, new constitutional damages cause, or Just Compensation Clause justification is adopted.

The action is not converted into an officer suit. Young and Coeur d'Alene require a connected officer, ongoing federal violation, proper cause, and genuinely prospective relief; Edelman and Pennhurst preserve retrospective-compensation and state-law boundaries. No Florida claims-bill or takings remedy is adjudged adequate or inadequate, and State Street supplies no invalidity finding about this different patent.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Union Gas:** Extends its retained five-vote Commerce-power constitutional conclusion into the Article I enforcement principle applied to the Patent Clause and Necessary and Proper Clause here. Its statutory holding and private third-party damages posture remain intact; its four-Justice complete rationale and absence of a Parden-waiver ground are not enlarged into historical majority holdings.
- **Seminole Tribe; Hans:** Seminole’s separation of explicit State-action authorization from constitutional amenability remains intact. Its set-aside of the State-dismissal direction established no controlling constitutional rationale, categorical Article I prohibition, abrogation-before-officer sequence, or overruling of Union Gas. The Court resolves the reserved power question here and limits Hans: its immunity does not prevent Congress from authorizing this federal-question action within the Article I enforcement principle.
- **Welch; Parden; Hilton:** Preserve the displacement of federal-forum consent inferred from general FELA/Jones Act coverage and Hilton's distinct specialized state-court FELA holding, including retained statutory construction and compensation reliance. Howlett's competent-state-court enforcement rule remains. Welch's fractured discussion did not adjudicate every later express prospective condition. Part IV does not reopen Hilton's federal action.
- **College Savings Bank:** Part IV applies its Part III holding, announced earlier the same day, that continued ordinary commerce is not unequivocal consent to federal suit, even under a clear prospective condition and a realistically optional undertaking. Its preservation of actual voluntary consent and its nonreach of litigation-waiver acts remain.
- **Fitzpatrick; Boerne:** Applies Fitzpatrick’s independent Section 5 authority and Boerne’s congruence-and-proportionality rule to sustain this preventive patent remedy. Congress may reach some conduct beyond independently unconstitutional deprivation without replacing the protected right. The holding adds no mandatory findings, evidentiary quantity, geographic restriction, or sunset requirement.
- **Daniels; Parratt; Hudson; Zinermon:** Preserve the distinction between property, conduct, process, and unconstitutional deprivation. The Court adopts no wholesale patent-negligence rule, no automatic willfulness exception, and no finding about Florida remedies. Hartford-Empire and Consolidated Fruit-Jar support patent property without deciding every infringement's constitutional character.
- **X-Citement Video; Izumi; Madsen; Lebron; Murphy:** Apply the unchanged-judgment respondent-defense route with its actual scope limits. Preservation, briefing, priority, or usefulness alone does not enlarge review. Kolstad and Olmstead supply included-ground comparisons, not transferred immunity or patent-remedy rules.
- **Schacht:** Preserves claim-specific immunity and possible waiver while leaving distinct litigation-waiver acts unresolved; it decides none of the acts asserted here.
- **Hess; Lane; Cedar Rapids; West; Blue Fox:** Retain their separate predicates of actual entity status, federal remedy-specific consent, accepted funding obligations, express federal compensation, and enacted remedial authority. None already resolves this State's commercial consent or eliminates its admitted arm status.
- **Puerto Rico Aqueduct:** Applies immediate review of a conclusive immunity denial without treating appealability as immunity, consent, or infringement.
- **Young; Coeur d'Alene; Edelman; Pennhurst:** Preserve the connected-officer, ongoing-federal-violation, genuinely prospective-relief, retrospective-compensation, and state-law boundaries; no officer claim is created.
- **State Street:** Its determination of the complete claims in that case establishes no categorical business-method exclusion or adjudication of Patent No.4,722,055.

**Limits and questions not reached:** This immunity-denial judgment stands and the covered patent action proceeds to ordinary merits adjudication, without a new constitutional-tort prerequisite imposed by the mandate. The express federal-forum and remedy provisions satisfy the distinct statutory-clarity inquiry, and patent property remains distinct from unconstitutional deprivation.

Section 5 and Article I independently sustain this action. Lower courts apply the congruent-and-proportional preventive-enforcement holding without requiring each patent plaintiff to prove a constitutional deprivation. They also apply the Article I rule when both the underlying obligation and enforcement measure fall within Congress’s enumerated authority and Congress unmistakably authorizes State exposure to suit. Union Gas’s constitutional conclusion is extended to this principle, and Hans supplies no immunity Congress cannot displace in this setting. Under Part IV, for all nine Justices, continued commercial participation after Congress authorizes suit is not consent, as College Savings Part III holds; actual consent through a distinct State choice, such as express waiver or voluntary invocation of federal jurisdiction, remains available, and whether Florida Prepaid's litigation conduct waived immunity remains undecided. Hilton’s state-forum holding and actual-waiver and officer-relief doctrines retain their distinct boundaries. The action proceeds without an actual-waiver adjudication as a prerequisite, and all merits and award-specific conditions remain for ordinary adjudication.

**Operative remedy or transition:** **Affirmed, 5–4.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.
**Opposing:** O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

The Federal Circuit's judgment sustaining denial of immunity remains in place. The case returns for ordinary adjudication of patent validity, infringement, causation, defenses, and lawful remedies. No liability, damages, trebling, fees, compulsory license, or injunction is awarded.

Part IV applies the controlling rejection of consent inferred from ordinary commercial participation in College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board, No. 98-149, June 23, 1999, in Federal Courts. The distinct judgments and affirmative rationales are not merged.


For the other questions in Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank, decided 1999-06-23, at the dockets stated above, see Business and Commercial Law.

### 1. The enacted fee language supplies no command repricing completed services

#### Martin v. Hadix

**Docket or dockets:** No. 98-262; October Term 1998.
**Decided:** 1999-06-21.

##### 1. The enacted fee language supplies no command repricing completed services

**Holding:** The general action-and-award language in original 42 U.S.C. §1997e(d) does not clearly direct application of its new hourly ceiling to legal services completed before April 26, 1996. The separate express transition for prospective relief in PLRA §802 does not itself establish the fee amendment's treatment of every pending action; each provision's operation must be examined.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas's Part I, directly joined by all nine Justices. The statement does not resolve whether the undertaking of representation or each service controls later work; that disagreement is addressed separately.

##### 2. The new ceiling does not govern pre-enactment services

**Holding:** The hourly ceiling in original §1997e(d)(3) does not govern legal services completed before the PLRA's April 26, 1996 enactment, even if the fee request or award follows enactment. Those services remain subject to the compensation law governing when they were performed.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas's Part II, directly joined by all nine Justices at this concrete level of application; broader methodological agreement is not inferred.

##### 3. Later services are subject to the ceiling despite the older action

**Holding:** Original §1997e(d)(3)'s hourly ceiling governs services performed after enactment in these already-pending prisoner actions. On remand, the district court must allocate compensation by when the legal work was actually performed, retaining the prior regime for completed pre-enactment work and applying the enacted ceiling to later work.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas's Part III, directly joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer. Stevens and Ginsburg dissent from this component. No Marks reconstruction supplies the rule.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Landgraf v. USI Film Products:** Applies the concrete inquiry into temporal direction and changed consequences for completed services. The existing general-framework holding retains its prior force and original coalition; this decision does not turn service-date agreement into new assent to every methodological proposition.
- **Rivers v. Roadway Express:** Preserves provision-specific construction; a statute's general remedial purpose does not supply retrospective reach for a particular amendment.
- **Kaiser Aluminum & Chemical Corp. v. Bonjorno:** Applies the priority of enacted temporal direction; its fee discussion does not establish an automatic rule choosing either action filing or fee-award date.
- **Bradley v. School Board of Richmond:** Retains its actual fee-legislation result; distinguishes it from using a new hourly ceiling to reduce compensation for already completed work. It is not overruled.
- **Lindh v. Murphy:** Preserves ordinary structural construction and its particular AEDPA application-date rule. The PLRA provisions' different functions prevent automatic transfer of that result to all fees in old prisoner actions.
- **Hughes Aircraft Co. v. United States ex rel. Schumer:** Preserves examination of the real legal change rather than a jurisdictional, procedural or collateral label. No new rule about its distinct private-enforcement facts is announced.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Before this decision the disputed pending-case application of the original PLRA hourly ceiling remained open. Effective June 21, 1999, the hourly-ceiling temporal rule requires service-date allocation in this setting: prior law for completed pre-enactment services and the new ceiling for later services, even in an older action. The original text's independent fee-entitlement and monetary-judgment provisions retain their own predicates and are not newly construed. No cited precedent is overruled; no final fee award is reopened.

**Operative remedy or transition:**

| Component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Remedy |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Services completed before April 26, 1996 | Exemption from the new hourly ceiling affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — | Compensation remains governed by the law applicable when the services were performed. |
| Services performed after enactment in these pending actions | Exemption reversed, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Stevens, Ginsburg | Apply the enacted hourly ceiling and allocate fees by actual service date on remand. |

The judgment is affirmed in part, reversed in part and remanded. The district court must allocate compensation by the dates of actual services, retaining the former compensation regime for completed pre-enactment work and applying the new hourly ceiling to later work. Actual compensable hours, amounts and independently applicable statutory predicates remain for ordinary lower proceedings. No final award, mandatory hearing, decree alteration, new prevailing-party finding or reopening of a completed fee judgment is ordered. Unaddressed constitutional objections remain undecided.

### The Eleventh Amendment does not bar review of Alabama's own judgment

#### South Central Bell Telephone Co. v. Alabama

**Docket or dockets:** No. 97-2045.
**Decided:** 1999-03-23.

##### The Eleventh Amendment does not bar review of Alabama's own judgment

**Holding:** The Eleventh Amendment does not prevent this Court from reviewing under 28 U.S.C. § 1257 the federal questions decided by Alabama's own courts. Such review is distinct from initiating a new federal damages action against the State.

**Proposition-level authority:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's unanimous opinion of the Court, Parts I–V.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** McKesson supplies the distinction between review of a state judgment and initiation of a federal action; it remains effective on that ground.

##### Shared taxpayer interests do not authorize binding these nonparties

**Holding:** Claim or issue preclusion cannot bind these taxpayers to earlier litigation merely because they share the earlier taxpayers' interests or were aware of the public dispute. Binding requires constitutionally sufficient consent, control, genuine privity, adequate class or other legally accountable representation, or another recognized relationship consistent with due process; the asserted common-interest rationale supplies none of those prerequisites.

**Proposition-level authority:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's unanimous opinion of the Court, Parts I–V.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Richards's due-process prerequisite is applied to both proposed forms of preclusion; its protection of lawful representative adjudication and ordinary precedent remains intact.

For the other questions and complete operative scope in South Central Bell Telephone Co. v. Alabama, 1999-03-23, No. 97-2045, see Federal Taxation.

### Private ADEA enforcement against state employers

#### Kimel v. Florida Board of Regents / United States v. Florida Board of Regents, 528 U.S. 62 (2000)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 98-791 and 98-796, consolidated.
**Decided:** January 11, 2000.

##### Whether the ADEA unmistakably authorizes these private federal actions against state employers

**Holding:** Sections 630(b), 626(b)–(c), and the incorporated FLSA provisions, read together, authorize private ADEA actions against state employers in federal court. That authorization establishes neither constitutional power, State consent, discrimination nor entitlement to relief.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter and Breyer; seven Justices.

**Material application:** Section 630(b) expressly covers States; section 626(b) incorporates FLSA remedies and procedures, excluding section 216(a); incorporated sections 216(b) and 203(x) include state public-agency employers and suit in federal or state court. Section 626(c) supplements rather than displaces that authorization.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 626(b) permits liquidated damages only for willful violations. Section 626(c)(1) ends the individual's right to bring the action when the EEOC commences an action enforcing that employee's right. The separately incorporated §216(b) terminates its action/party right upon the Secretary's §217 complaint seeking restraint of delay in the employee's unpaid minimum wages or overtime, or legal or equitable relief for alleged violations of §215(a)(3), the prohibition on retaliation. These distinct provisions are not collapsed into one identical rule, and no such governmental filing is adjudicated here.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Seminole Tribe and Florida Prepaid separate unmistakable authorization from constitutional power; combined enactments satisfy the former question here.


##### Whether Article I sustains the expressly authorized ADEA enforcement measure

**Holding:** Congress may enforce the valid federal age-employment duty against States through this expressly authorized private federal action when both the duty and its enforcement measure fall within Congress's Article I authority. The Eleventh Circuit's categorical immunity dismissal therefore cannot stand.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Parts II–III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Breyer; five Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court reserves the ADEA's §5 validity. Age classifications receive rational-basis constitutional review, while the statute supplies broader protection; this distinction neither establishes a §5 holding nor imposes an individual constitutional-tort prerequisite. Coverage, defenses, proof and award-specific statutory and constitutional limits remain for ordinary adjudication. No officer action, state exhaustion requirement or universal authorization of every federal remedy is created.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the immunity dismissals in both dockets and remand for ordinary adjudication. No discrimination, defense, back-pay entitlement, liquidated award, fees or other relief is established.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank, Part III, is applied to the ADEA's authorized employment-enforcement measure; its Article I rule remains controlling beyond its patent application. Pennsylvania v. Union Gas retains the constitutional conclusion extended by that rule. Seminole Tribe's distinction between authorization and power is applied without a categorical Article I prohibition. EEOC v. Wyoming and Garcia supply the valid commerce basis for covered state-employment regulation. Alden preserves its separate competent-state-court duty without becoming an exhaustion condition. College Savings Bank preserves the rejection of consent inferred from ordinary activity. Boerne and Florida Prepaid's §5 holdings remain effective; neither is used to decide the reserved ADEA §5 question. Gregory's rational-basis age rule is not a ceiling on otherwise valid commerce legislation.

### Tax refund and prospective-remedy thresholds

#### Browne v. United States

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-632; Second Circuit No. 98-6124.

**Decided:** March 27, 2000.

For the separate RFRA rules governing the collected additions in Browne, March 27, 2000, No. 99-632, see Federal Taxation — Religious objections to tax collection and additions.

##### Whether the RFRA claim for collected interest can be decided without classifying it and resolving preserved threshold objections

**Holding:** The merits rejection of the independent RFRA claim for recovery of collected interest is vacated because the lower courts did not distinguish that claim from discretionary interest-abatement review or decide the preserved jurisdiction and remedy objections. The lower courts must classify the claim and resolve those objections before its disputed merits; the Court decides neither the available forum nor the RFRA entitlement to recover interest.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part II, supported by all eight Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela dissents from this part.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The section 1346(a)(1) refund channel, section 7422 administrative prerequisite, section 6404 review provision and RFRA appropriate-relief language remain distinct. The Court finds neither district-court jurisdiction nor its exclusion, and does not classify every statutory restriction as an Article III limit. Section 6601(h) separates failure to pay estimated tax from interest on principal; no combined computation or interest merits rule follows.

##### Whether section 6404 discretionary-abatement review may be merged with the RFRA refund theory

**Holding:** The section 6404 abuse-of-discretion theory is remanded for decision of its preserved review-channel, timing and remedial questions before any merits. The Court leaves open the exclusivity of the Tax Court route, when the operative request was made and whether the statutory conditions for review or abatement are met.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part III-A, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The review provision, originally section 6404(g) and now (i), has a claimant-eligibility cross-reference to section 7430(c)(4) and a 180-day period after mailing of final refusal. The operative request date, temporal applicability, satisfaction of those conditions and asserted Tax Court exclusivity remain unresolved. No tolling entitlement, new filing period or statutory abatement ground is established.

##### Whether refund merits decide authority for the requested prospective tax remedies

**Holding:** Before deciding the merits of the requested declaration, practice-and-exemption-access order or compulsory-levy order without interest or penalties, the lower courts must decide the preserved jurisdiction and remedy objections, including the federal-tax restrictions in 28 U.S.C. section 2201(a) and 26 U.S.C. section 7421(a). RFRA's authorization of appropriate relief does not itself establish authority to grant those remedies.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part III-B, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Standing, sovereign immunity, the judicial channel and the distinct federal-tax declaration and injunction restrictions must be addressed for the relief actually requested. The Court adopts no exception to section 2201(a) or section 7421(a), no general immunity waiver and no rule that every compulsory-collection accommodation fails.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate the RFRA collected-interest determination 8–1 and the section 6404 and prospective-relief determinations unanimously. Remand for the identified threshold determinations before disputed merits. No transfer, refund, abatement, compelled levy, practice change, exemption-access order or automatic evidence-taking is required.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Steel Co. preserves the constitutional boundary against disputed-merits adjudication on assumed necessary power. Ruhrgas permits bounded nonmerits sequencing without disregarding statutory commands or requiring a universal order among thresholds. Izumi preserves the grant boundary; resolving preserved threshold objections does not grant the independent constitutional or Code merits.

### Assigned government claims and statutory State-defendant coverage

#### Vermont Agency of Natural Resources v. United States ex rel. Stevens, 529 U.S. 765 (2000)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 98-1828.
**Decided:** May 22, 2000.

##### I. Transfer of the government's financial claim supplies standing

**Holding:** The FCA's partial assignment of the United States' concrete financial claim gives the relator standing to enforce that assigned claim, subject to the ordinary injury, traceability and redressability requirements at the litigation stage. A prospective reward alone would not create injury, and this assignment conclusion does not itself make the relator's action the United States' own suit for every other legal purpose.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Scalia's opinion is joined by all eight other Justices, giving nine direct votes.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Standing leaves defendant coverage, constitutional immunity and Article II control separate. The Court does not settle every assignment, penalty-only suit or private public-interest action. The pleaded federal financial loss remains subject to the proof required at each litigation stage; no fraud is found.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Riley v. St. Luke's Episcopal Hospital, January 9, 2002, resolves the reserved Article II questions for the FCA's bounded assigned-claim mechanism after governmental declination: its actual retained governmental powers satisfy the Take Care Clause, and its case-specific relator occupies no continuing federal office. Vermont Agency's assigned-claim standing and sovereign-State defendant holdings remain unchanged.

##### II. This private-relator action is not authorized against a sovereign State

**Holding:** Section 3729's generic person category, read with the FCA's private-relator structure and section 3733's investigation-specific definition, contains no affirmative indication authorizing this privately maintained action against a sovereign State. The action against the Vermont Agency must therefore be dismissed on statutory grounds without deciding constitutional State immunity.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II is joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer with Scalia, seven direct votes for this complete bounded ground. Stevens and Souter dissent.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court leaves open suits brought by the United States, municipal liability, retaliation claims, differently drafted legislation and Article II questions. It decides neither ultimate fraud nor any remedy amount. Its rule is confined to this private-relator action against the State.

**Material application:** The United States' appellate participation did not take over the relator's action. Receipt of federal grants establishes no consent; the decision supplies no categorical Article I immunity bar.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The case returns through the Second Circuit for dismissal of the private-relator action against the Vermont Agency on the statutory ground. The dismissal does not adjudicate the alleged fraud, the amount of loss or constitutional immunity and authorizes no recovery.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife:** Applied to require a real injury and litigation-stage support; an assigned government financial claim supplies a different basis from bare enforcement permission.
- **Citizens for a Better Environment v. Steel Co.:** Applied by deciding Article III before contested statutory coverage.
- **Friends of the Earth v. Laidlaw:** Distinguished from a claim that reward or deterrence alone creates injury; its independent-injury requirement remains.
- **Florida Prepaid v. College Savings Bank:** Its Article I enforcement rule and distinct unmistakable-authorization requirement remain controlling; congressional power alone supplies no FCA coverage.
- **Kimel v. Florida Board of Regents:** Applied to keep statutory clarity separate from constitutional power; its express authorization does not decide this differently drafted statute.
- **College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid:** Its rejection of consent inferred from ordinary continued activity remains; no consent is found from receipt of grants.

### Automatic stays of prospective prison relief

#### Miller v. French / United States v. French, 530 U.S. 327 (2000)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 99-224 and 99-582.
**Decided:** June 19, 2000.

##### I. The automatic stay displaces ordinary equitable continuation

**Holding:** For the presented motion under 18 U.S.C. §3626(b)(2), subsection (e) automatically stays covered prospective relief beginning on the thirtieth day after filing and ending when the court enters a final order resolving the motion; the court may postpone the starting date for good cause by no more than sixty days, but general calendar congestion is not good cause. Beyond that express postponement, ordinary equitable authority does not permit the court to suspend the statutory stay.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Justice Souter's opinion of the Court has six direct rationale votes: O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela joins the judgment without joining this statutory-construction part; Stevens and Breyer dissent from the construction.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 3626(e)(4) makes orders staying, suspending, delaying or barring the automatic stay appealable as refusals to dissolve or modify an injunction under 28 U.S.C. §1292(a)(1), regardless of their label or preliminary or final character; it supplies review, not additional substantive authority to block the stay. The express subsection (e)(3) postponement is excluded from that special appeal treatment. Constitutional avoidance cannot replace this detailed statutory direction with a competing scheme. Subsection (e)(1)'s prompt-disposition duty and mandamus remedy remain. The retained 180-day timing language for motions under other law does not govern the immediate-termination motion presented here; its broader interaction with the amended under-subsection-(b) opening is not decided. Section 3626(a)(2)'s separate ninety-day expiration for newly entered preliminary relief does not turn this existing permanent decree into a preliminary order.

##### III. The remand preserves a genuine adjudication of supported application objections

**Holding:** In each docket, the Seventh Circuit's categorical invalidation is reversed and the case remanded, permitting a properly supported claim that the statutory timing, including available postponement, actually denies a meaningful opportunity to adjudicate to be presented and decided under Article III as well as any applicable due-process protection. No such violation is presumed, no feasibility hearing is required merely because time has elapsed, and no ordinary equitable exception or automatic continuation of the existing blocking injunction follows.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg; seven Justices directly join this operative remand and its limits.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Benjamin requires a real opportunity to offer material current proof when it is necessary for statutory adjudication; Gavin and Hadix require the unperformed termination inquiry without making a new hearing universal. The reported postponements of the termination hearing establish neither constitutional impossibility nor prison compliance, present violations or deliberate delay. They therefore neither compel an evidentiary hearing about timing nor foreclose a supported application objection. On remand, such an objection must receive genuine consideration under ordinary procedure; this judgment does not restrict it to due process or decide the appropriate remedy in advance. The court must also decide the actual termination conditions: the movant must establish that covered relief was entered without substantive necessity, narrowness and least-intrusion findings, and sufficient findings require no ritual wording. Under amended §3626(b)(3), relief shall not terminate if written, record-based findings establish that it remains necessary to correct a current and ongoing federal-right violation, extends no further than necessary, is narrowly drawn and is the least intrusive necessary means. Each retained duty requires those findings; obsolete relief may not survive through disregard of the new law. This appeal neither decides those merits nor terminates the decree. Hadix's distinct pending automatic-stay appeal is not adjudicated here.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The categorical Article III invalidation cannot sustain the blocking injunction. The remand permits a supported inability-to-adjudicate objection under Article III as well as due process to be presented and decided; it neither automatically continues the blocking order nor denies consideration of a properly supported constitutional claim. No prison-compliance finding or termination of the underlying decree is entered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Inmates of Suffolk County Jail v. Rouse:** Applied to lawful changed prospective standards, actual findings and preservation of completed effects; its rejection of speculative compulsory inquiry remains.
- **Benjamin v. Jacobson:** Applied to genuine opportunity for necessary current proof; its documented refusal of proof is not presumed from this hearing calendar.
- **Gavin v. Branstad:** Applied to the actual statutory inquiry still required after rejection of a constitutional ground; it commands neither unconditional continuation nor a hearing in every case.
- **Hadix v. Johnson:** Applied to independent adjudication of amended termination conditions; its separate automatic-stay appeal remains distinct.
- **Plyler v. Moore:** Its original-text conditional termination rule and separate statutory route remain; the amended current-and-ongoing conjunction is not retroactively written into its holding.
- **Lewis v. Casey:** Its scope and remedial-findings safeguards remain; the existing decree receives actual substantive examination rather than a ritual-words test.
- **Rufo v. Inmates of Suffolk County Jail:** Its independent significant-change and tailored-modification route remains; it adds no prerequisite to the statutory termination route.

For the bounded Article III holding, see Miller v. French / United States v. French, Nos. 99-224 and 99-582, June 19, 2000, under Constitutional Structure, legislative change to prospective relief.

### Defendant-specific immunity in election-withdrawal litigation

#### Mixon v. Ohio

**Docket or dockets:** Sixth Circuit No. 98-3368; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.
